Outlier-robust additive matrix decomposition
Philip Thompson

Abstract
We study least-squares trace regression when the parameter is the sum of a r r -low-rank matrix and a s s -sparse matrix and a fraction ϵ \epsilon of the labels is corrupted. For subgaussian distributions and feature-dependent noise, we highlight three needed design properties, each one derived from a different process inequality: a “product process inequality”, “Chevet’s inequality” and a “multiplier process inequality”. These properties handle, simultaneously, additive decomposition, label contamination and design-noise interaction. They imply the near-optimality of a tractable estimator with respect to the effective dimensions d eff , r d_{\eff,r} and d eff , s d_{\eff,s} of the low-rank and sparse components, ϵ \epsilon and the failure probability δ \delta . The near-optimal rate is 𝗋 ⁡ ( n , d eff , r ) + 𝗋 ⁡ ( n , d eff , s ) + ( 1 + log ⁡ ( 1 / δ ) ) / n + ϵ ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / ϵ ) , \mathsf{r}(n,d_{\eff,r})+\mathsf{r}(n,d_{\eff,s})+\sqrt{(1+\log(1/\delta))/n}+\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon), where 𝗋 ⁡ ( n , d eff , r ) + 𝗋 ⁡ ( n , d eff , s ) \mathsf{r}(n,d_{\eff,r})+\mathsf{r}(n,d_{\eff,s}) is the optimal rate in average with no contamination. Our estimator is adaptive to ( s , r , ϵ , δ ) (s,r,\epsilon,\delta) and, for fixed absolute constant c > 0 c>0 , it attains the mentioned rate with probability 1 − δ 1-\delta uniformly over all δ ≥ exp ⁡ ( − c ​ n ) \delta\geq\exp(-cn) . Without matrix decomposition, our analysis also entails optimal bounds for a robust estimator adapted to the noise variance. Our estimators are based on “sorted” versions of Huber’s loss. We present simulations matching the theory. In particular, it reveals the superiority of “sorted” Huber’s losses over the classical Huber’s loss.



1
Introduction

Outlier-robust estimation has been a topic studied for many decades since the seminal work by Huber
[
49
]
.
One of the objectives of the field is to device estimators which are less sensitive to outlier contamination. The formalization of outlyingness and the construction of robust estimators matured in several directions. One common assumption is that the adversary can only change a fraction
\epsilon
of the original sample. For an extensive overview we refer, e.g., to

[
45
,
62
,
50
]
and references therein.

Within a very general framework, the minimax optimality of several robust estimation problems has been recently obtained in a series of elegant works by
[
15
,
16
,
44
]
. The construction, however, is based on Tukey’s depth, a hard computational problem in higher dimensions. A recent trend of research, initiated by
[
32
,
55
]
, has focused on the optimality of robust estimators within computationally tractable algorithms. The
oblivious
model assumes the contamination is independent of the original sample. In the
adversarial
model, the outliers may depend arbitrarily on the sample. For instance, optimal mean estimators for the adversarial model can now be computed in nearly-linear time
[
21
,
38
,
30
,
28
]
. We refer to
[
33
]
for an extensive survey.

In the realm of robust linear regression, two broad lines of investigations exist: (1) one in which only the response (label) is contaminated and (2) the more general setting in which the covariates (features) are also corrupted
[
34
,
36
]
. Model (1), albeit less general, has been considered in many applications and studied in numerous past and recent works
[
12
,
59
,
82
,
43
,
74
,
35
]
. It has also some connection with the problems of robust matrix completion
[
13
,
19
,
17
,
59
,
52
]
and matrix decomposition
[
14
,
13
,
91
,
48
,
2
]
.
Both models (1)-(2) have been considered assuming adversarial or oblivious contamination. For instance, an interesting property of model (1) with oblivious contamination is the existence of consistent estimators, a property not shared by the adversary model. See for instance
[
85
,
8
,
82
,
43
,
74
]
.

In this work, we consider a new model: robust trace regression with additive matrix decomposition (RTRMD). It corresponds to least-squares trace regression when the parameter is the sum of a low-rank matrix and a sparse matrix and, simultaneously, the labels are adversarially contaminated. We assume there are at most
o
outliers and the sample size
n
is much smaller than the extrinsic dimension
p
. We focus on subgaussian distributions and pay attention to the following points:

(a)

Adversarial label corruption in high dimensions.
The parameter is a
d_{1}\times d_{2}
matrix and
n\ll p:=d_{1}d_{2}
. RTRMD includes, as particular cases,
s
-sparse linear regression
[
84
]
and noisy
r
-low-rank matrix sensing
[
68
]
. One practical appeal of the established theory of high-dimensional estimation is the existence of efficient estimators adapted to

(s,r)
— without resorting to Lepski’s method. Likewise, we assume no knowledge of
(s,r,o)
.

(b)

Noise heterogeneity.
The majority of the literature on (robust) least-squares regression, within the framework of
M
-estimation with decomposable regularizers
[
69
]
, assumes feature-independent noise. We avoid this assumption and identify design properties and concentration inequalities needed for this case. See Section
9
for a discussion.

(c)

Subgaussian rates and uniform confidence level.
Minimax rates are defined on average or as a function of the failure probability
\delta
. For instance, the first seminal bounds in sparse linear regression were of the form
\sqrt{s\log(p/s\delta)/n}
— optimal in average but suboptimal in
\delta
. The optimal rate is the “subgaussian” rate
\sqrt{s\log(p/s)/n}+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n}
— for which
\log(1/\delta)
does not multiply the ‘‘effective dimension’’.
1
1

1

The decoupling of
\log(1/\delta)
with the dimension is of major concern in the recent literature of heavy-tailed estimation
[
2019lugosi:mendelson-survey
]
. There are significant additional challenges. For instance, the optimal estimator depends on
\delta

[
2016devroye:lerasle:lugosi:oliveira
]
.
A second point is to what extent the estimator
depends on
\delta
. Let
c>0
be an absolute constant.
Without knowing
\delta
, is there an estimator that “automatically” attains the optimal rate
across all

\delta\geq\exp(-cn)
with probability at least
1-\delta
? For sparse linear regression,
[
6
]
was the first to answer these points affirmatively when the
noise is independent of the features
. We ask the same questions for the general model RTRMD in case the noise is feature-dependent. See Section
9
for a discussion.

(d)

Matrix decomposition.
It is considered in
[
89
,
14
,
13
,
91
,
48
,
63
]
assuming the “incoherence” condition and in
[
2
]
assuming the milder “low-spikeness” condition. See also Chapter 7 of the book
[
47
]
. These works do not consider label contamination. A general framework is proposed in
[
2
]
assuming a specific design property (see their Definition 2). In the applications considered in
[
2
]
, this property is straightforwardly satisfied: either the design is the identity or the fixed design is invertible. For instance, multi-task learning has an invertible design in high dimensions (one has
n\geq d_{1}
albeit
n\ll d_{1}d_{2}
). In this regard, trace regression with additive matrix decomposition is fundamentally a different model: with high probability, the random design is singular in the regime
n\ll d_{1}d_{2}
. To our knowledge, there is currently no optimal statistical theory for RTRMD — even without label contamination. Under assumptions (a)-(c), this work identifies three design properties to prove optimality for this problem:

\PP
,
\IP
and
\MP
. Respectively, each one is derived from a different process inequality: a “product process inequality”, “Chevet’s inequality” and a “multiplier process inequality”. See Sections
6.3
and
8
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. We start presenting some useful notation. Section
2
states our general framework, Section
3
presents our estimators and Section
4
exemplifies with concrete models. Section
5
state new results for these models. It also presents reference to later sections concerning technical contributions. In Section
6
, we review related literature and compare with our results. In Section
7
, we state two concentration inequalities for the multiplier and product processes. In Section
8
, we state our needed design properties and apply these inequalities to prove them. Section
9
presents a preliminary discussion. Sections
10
-
11
presents the proofs of our main results, namely, Theorems
15
and
21
. Finally, Sections
12
and
13
finish with simulations and a final discussion. Additional proofs are presented in the Supplemental Material.

Notation
.

We set
\mathds{R}^{p}:=\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
. For a vector
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{k}
denotes its
\ell_{k}
-norm (
1\leq k\leq\infty
) and

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{0}
is the number of its nonzero entries. We use similar notation for matrices (considered as vectors). We denote the Frobenius norm by
\|\cdot\|_{F}
, the nuclear norm by
\|\cdot\|_{N}
and the operator norm by
\|\cdot\|_{\op}
. Given norm
\mathcal{R}
and

\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}\setminus\{0\}
,

\Psi_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{V}):=\nicefrac{{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})}}{{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{F}}}.

The inner product in
\mathds{R}^{p}
will be denoted by
\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{W})
. For vectors, we use the notation

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. If
a\leq Cb
for some absolute constant
C>0
, we write
a\lesssim b
or
a\leq\mathcal{O}(b)
. We write
a\asymp b
if
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a
. Given
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

[\ell]:=\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
. The

\psi_{2}
-Orlicz norm will be denoted by
|\cdot|_{\psi_{2}}
. Throughout the paper, given
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
A^{(\ell)}:=A/\sqrt{\ell}
whenever
A
is a number, vector or function.

We now recall the definition of the Slope norm
[
11
]
. Given nonincreasing positive sequence
\boldsymbol{\omega}:=\{\omega_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, the Slope norm at a point

\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined by

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\sharp}:=\sum_{i\in[n]}\omega_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\sharp},

where
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\sharp}\geq\ldots\geq\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\sharp}
denotes the nonincreasing rearrangement of the absolute coordinates of
\boldsymbol{u}
. Throughout this paper, unless otherwise stated,
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
will be the sequence with coordinates
\omega_{i}=\sqrt{\log(An/i)}
for some
A\geq 2
. Recall that
\Omega:=\{\sum_{i=1}^{o}\omega_{i}^{2}\}^{1/2}\asymp o\log(n/o)

[
6
]
. With some abuse of notation,

\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
will also denote the Slope norm in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
with sequence
\bar{w}_{j}=\sqrt{\log(\bar{A}p/j)}
for some
\bar{A}\geq 2
.

\mathbf{X}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
will denote a random matrix with distribution
\Pi
and covariance operator
\mathfrak{S}
— seen as a vector,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is its covariance matrix. Given
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define the bilinear form

\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}:=\mathbb{E}[\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle]=\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{V}),\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle

and the pseudo-norms

\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}:=\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}^{1/2}
,

\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}:=\{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}^{2}+\|\mathbf{W}\|_{\Pi}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}\}^{1/2}
,

\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}]\|_{\Pi}:=\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\mathbf{0}]\|_{\Pi}

and

\|[\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}:=\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}
.
Given
\mathbb{C}\subset\mathds{R}^{p}
, let

\mu(\mathbb{C}):=\sup_{\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{C}}\nicefrac{{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{F}}}{{\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}}}.

Next, we define the unit balls

\mathbb{B}_{\Pi}:=\{\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}:\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}\leq 1\},

\mathbb{B}_{F}:=\{\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}:\|\mathbf{V}\|_{F}\leq 1\},

\mathbb{B}_{\ell}^{k}:=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\ell}\leq 1\}

and, for given norm
\mathcal{R}
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}}:=\{\boldsymbol{v}:\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v})\leq 1\}
.
All the corresponding unit spheres will take the symbol
\mathbb{S}
.
Finally, the
Gaussian width
of a compact set

\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k\times\ell}
is the quantity

\mathscr{G}(\mathcal{B}):=\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{B}}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{\Xi}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle],

where
\boldsymbol{\Xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times\ell}
is random matrix with iid
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries.

2
General framework

Assumption 1

(Adversarial label contamination)
.

Let
\{(y_{i}^{\circ},\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\circ})\}_{i\in[n]}
be an iid copy of a feature-label pair
(\mathbf{X},y)\in\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}
. We assume available a sample

\{(y_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
such that

\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\circ}
for all
i\in[n]
and at most
o
arbitrary outliers modify the label sample
\{y_{i}^{\circ}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

Letting
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{i})_{i\in[n]}
, our underlying model is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{f}+\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\xi},

(2)

where
\boldsymbol{f}:=(f_{i})_{i\in[n]}
and

\boldsymbol{\xi}:=(\xi_{i})_{i\in[n]}
are, respectively, iid copies of unknown random variables
f,\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an arbitrary unknown vector with at most
o
nonzero coordinates.
2
2

2

Nothing more is assumed on
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
. For instance, it can depend arbitrarily on the data set. We are not concerned with
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
itself and rather see it as a nuisance parameter.

We assume
\xi
and
\mathbf{X}
are centered and

\mathbb{E}[\xi\mathbf{X}]=0
. The number

\epsilon:=o/n
is referred as the “contamination fraction”.

Define the
design operator

\mathfrak{X}:\mathds{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
with components

\mathfrak{X}_{i}(\mathbf{V}):=\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle
. In its general form, our goal is to estimate

\boldsymbol{f}
assuming that, for some
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]\in\mathds{R}^{p}
, the average approximation error

(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\|\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}

is “small”. To be precise, assuming
\epsilon\leq c
for some constant
c\in(0,1/2)
, we would like to design an estimator
[\hat{\mathbf{B}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
satisfying, with probability at least
1-\delta
, “oracle inequalities” of the form

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\mathbf{B}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\inf_{[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]\in\mathcal{F}}\left\{C\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}+r_{\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(n,d_{\eff},\epsilon,\delta)\right\}.

(3)

In above,
C\approx 1
is an universal constant,
\mathcal{F}
is a class of parameters associated to well known parsimonious properties — e.g, sparsity or low-rankness — and
r_{\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(n,d_{\eff},\epsilon,\delta)
is an appropriate rate that depends on the “effective dimension”
d_{\eff}
of
\mathcal{F}
. In Section
5
, we specify different classes of interest, each associated to the examples of Section
4
.

When the model is “well-specified”, there is
[\mathbf{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}]\in\mathcal{F}
such that

\displaystyle[\mathbf{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}]\in\argmin_{[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[y-\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X},\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\right]^{2}.

(4)

This corresponds to (
2
) with
f:=\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X},\mathbf{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle
. In this case,

\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}=0
, Assumption
1
and (
2
) are equivalent and the rate
r_{\mathbf{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}}(n,d_{\eff},\epsilon,\delta)
is the minimax optimal rate for the well-specified model. More broadly, our goal is to obtain, under the assumptions discussed in the introduction, inequalities of the form (
3
), ensuring optimal statistical guarantees for least-squares trace regression in presence of either additive matrix decomposition, label contamination or inexact parsimony.

3
Our estimators

Our estimators are based on the following class of losses.

Definition 1

(Sorted Huber-type losses)
.

Define the functions

\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}

and

\rho_{2}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}

over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For
q\in\{1,2\}
and
\tau>0
, let

\rho_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},q}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the infimal convolution of
\rho_{q}
and
\tau\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
, i.e.,

\rho_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},q}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\min_{\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{q}(\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{z})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\sharp}.

Finally, define the loss

\mathcal{L}_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},q}(\mathbf{B}):=\rho_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},q}\left(\nicefrac{{\boldsymbol{y}-\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{B})}}{{\sqrt{n}}}\right).

These losses are convex. For instance, when
q=2
,
\rho_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},2}
is the optimal value of the problem defining the proximal mapping of
\tau\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
. When

\omega_{1}=\ldots=\omega_{n}=1
, separability implies the explicit expression:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},2}(\mathbf{B})=\tau^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi\left(\frac{y_{i}-\mathfrak{X}_{i}(\mathbf{B})}{\tau\sqrt{n}}\right),

(5)

where
\Phi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the Huber’s function
\Phi(t)=\min\{(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})t^{2},|t|-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\}
. Thus, Huber regression corresponds to
M
-estimation with the loss
\mathcal{L}_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},2}
with
constant
weighting sequence
\boldsymbol{\omega}
. In this work we advocate the use of the loss

\mathcal{L}_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},2}
with
varying weights
. Throughout this paper, we fix the sequence

\boldsymbol{\omega}:=(\omega_{i})_{i\in[n]}
to be

\omega_{i}:=\sqrt{\log(An/i)}

for some
A\geq 2
. It corresponds to a “Sorted” generalization of Huber’s loss.

In high-dimensions, we additionally use regularization norms
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S})
. We consider the estimator

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccl}[\hat{\mathbf{B}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]&\in&\argmin_{[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},2}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})+\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})+\chi\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\\
&&\mbox{s.t.}\quad\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathsf{a},\end{array}

where
\lambda,\chi,\tau>0
and
\mathsf{a}\in(0,\infty]
are tuning parameters. If we are not concerned with matrix decomposition, we instead consider, for
q\in\{1,2\}
, estimators of the form

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccl}\hat{\mathbf{B}}&\in&\argmin_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}}\mathcal{L}_{\tau\boldsymbol{\omega},q}(\mathbf{B})+\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}).\end{array}

By adding an extra variable
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
— aiming in estimating the nuisance parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
— the solution
of (
3
) can be equivalently found solving

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccl}&&\min_{[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\boldsymbol{\theta}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle+\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\right)^{2}+\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})+\chi\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\sharp}\\
&&\mbox{s.t.}\quad\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathsf{a}.\end{array}

Estimator (
3
) is a concrete example of regularization with three norms.

Similarly, the solution of (
3
) with
q=2
can be found solving

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccl}&&\min_{[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\theta}]\in\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{B}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle+\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\right)^{2}+\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\sharp}.\end{array}

The practical appeal of these problems is that they can be computed by alternated convex optimization using standard Lasso, Slope or nuclear norm solvers
[
11
]
.

Finally, the solution of (
3
) with
q=1
can be found solving

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccl}&&\min_{[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\theta}]\in\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{B}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle+\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\right)^{2}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\sharp}.\end{array}

When
\boldsymbol{\theta}\equiv\mathbf{0}
and
\mathcal{R}=\|\cdot\|_{1}
, the above estimator corresponds to the square-root lasso estimator
[
7
]
. When not concerned with matrix decomposition, we will show that the robust estimator (
3
) achieves the same guarantees as (
3
) — under the same set of assumptions (a)-(c) in Section
1
— while being adaptive to the variance of the noise. Of course, the computational cost of (
3
) is higher.

Remark 1

(Huber loss versus “Sorted” Huber loss)
.

Let us illustrate considering the well-specified model with

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}\equiv\mathbf{0}
and
q=2
. It is well known that, in linear regression, Huber’s estimator can be cast as a least-squares estimator in the augmented variable

[\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\theta}]
with the penalization
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{1}

[
80
,
39
]
.
In this work, we penalize
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\sharp}
— i.e., we fit with the loss in Definition
1
. Figure
1
plots the estimation error as a function of
\epsilon
in sparse linear regression using synthetic contaminated data. We refer to Section
12
for details. We can see that the “Sorted” Huber loss significantly outperforms Huber regression. In this work, we present near-optimal bounds trying to explain this significant empirical observation. Roughly, the intuition is that the former loss assigns more weight to outliers with larger magnitude. Huber regression processes all label data points indistinguishably.

Figure 1
:
Huber vs “Sorted” Huber losses in sparse regression:

\sqrt{\texttt{MSE}}
versus
\epsilon
.

4
Motivating examples

Matrix decomposition is motivated by several applications. We refer to
[
2
]
and Chapter 7 of the book
[
47
]
for a precise discussion and further references. They consider the general framework: to estimate the pair

[\mathbf{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}
given a noisy linear observation of its sum. Precisely, their model is

\mathbf{Y}=\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*})+\boldsymbol{\Xi}

where the design
\mathfrak{X}:\mathds{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
takes values on matrices with
n
iid rows and
\boldsymbol{\Xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
is a noise matrix independent of
\mathfrak{X}
with
n
centered iid rows. It is further required that
\mathbf{B}^{*}
is low-rank,

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}
is a sparse matrix and that a “low-spikeness” conditions holds. Three subproblems are analyzed in the framework of
[
2
]
: factor analysis, robust covariance estimation and multi-task learning. The first two problems have identity designs (
\mathfrak{X}=I
). In multi-task learning, the design is invertible with high probability.
Trace regression with additive matrix decomposition corresponds to their model with
m=1
and design

\mathfrak{X}_{i}(\mathbf{B}):=\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{B}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle
. Alternatively, it corresponds to (
2
) assuming the well-specified case,

\rank(\mathbf{B}^{*})\leq r

and
\|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}\|_{0}\leq s
and

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\equiv\mathbf{0}
. As discussed in item (d) of the introduction, the design property in
[
2
]
is not guaranteed to hold for this problem. More broadly, we consider additive matrix decomposition in trace regression when labels are contaminated.

To finish, we require the so called “low-spikeness” assumption: there exists
\mathsf{a}^{*}>0
such that for any potential parameter

\mathbf{B}
,

\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}\leq\nicefrac{{\mathsf{a}^{*}}}{{\sqrt{n}}}.

For this problem, we consider estimator (
3
) with tuning

\mathsf{a}:=\mathsf{a}^{*}/\sqrt{n}
,
\mathcal{R}:=\|\cdot\|_{N}
and
\mathcal{S}:=\|\cdot\|_{1}
. Two well known particular submodels of RTRMD are:

a)

Sparse regression

[
86
,
10
]
is a particular case of model (
4
) with
d_{1}=p
,
d_{2}=1
,

\boldsymbol{x}:=\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}:=\mathbf{B}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}\equiv\mathbf{0}
and
\|\boldsymbol{b}^{*}\|_{0}\leq s\ll p
. In case of label contamination (
0\neq\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{0}\leq o
), we consider estimators (
3
) with
q\in\{1,2\}
and

\mathcal{R}:=\|\cdot\|_{1}
or
\mathcal{R}=\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
.

b)

Trace regression
(or matrix sensing)
[
77
,
76
,
78
,
68
]
correspond to model (
4
) with

\mathbf{B}^{*}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
having rank
r\ll d_{1}\wedge d_{2}
and

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}\equiv\mathbf{0}
. With contaminated labels, we consider estimators (
3
) with
q\in\{1,2\}
and

\mathcal{R}:=\|\cdot\|_{N}
.

5
Results for RTRMD and robust sparse/low-rank regression

We state in this section optimal guarantees for the estimators (
3
)-(
3
) of models of Section
4
. These are in fact particular consequences of our main results: Theorems
6
-
7
, Proposition
2
, Theorem
15
and Theorem
21
. Being somewhat technical, we state them, respectively, in the later sections: Sections
7
,
8
,
10
and
11
. As a prelude to the proofs in Sections
10
-
11
, we include Section
9
. It discusses the role of the multiplier process inequality (Theorem
6
in Section
7
) within the framework of
M
-estimation with decomposable regularizers. Theorem
15
, the main result of the paper, is a general deterministic result for estimator (
3
) and RTRMD. It assumes specific design properties presented in Definition
8
in Section
8
. Proposition
2
in this section ensures these properties are satisfied with high probability. Its proof requires concentration inequalities stated in Theorems
6
-
7
of Section
7
. Theorem
21
in Section
11
is a general deterministic result for estimator (
3
) with
q=1
and the problem of robust trace regression (with no matrix decomposition). This theorem ensures this estimator is near-optimal adaptively to the noise variance. The proof of Theorem
21
is largely inspired by the proof of Theorem
15
. These points are explained in detail later.

Next, we work with distributions satisfying the following assumption.

Assumption 2
.

\mathbf{X}
is centered and
L
-subgaussian for some
L\geq 1
, that is,

|\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle|_{\psi_{2}}\leq L\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}

for all
\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
. Additionally,
1\leq|\xi|_{\psi_{2}}=:\sigma<\infty
.

Define

\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}):=\max_{j\in[p]}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jj}^{1/2},\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}):=\sup_{\|[\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}]\|_{2}=1}\{\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{v})^{2}\}^{1/2}.

Throughout the paper, we let
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
,

\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}:=\widehat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}

and

\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma},

where
[\hat{\mathbf{B}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}]
denote estimators and
\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
are given points.

Next, we give guarantees for the estimator (
3
) and RTRMD. Given
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathsf{a}^{*},\mathsf{c}>0
, we define the class

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(r,s,\mathsf{a}^{*},\mathsf{c})

\displaystyle:=\left\{[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}:\begin{array}[]{c}\rank(\mathbf{B})\leq r,\|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\|_{0}\leq s,\\
(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\|\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\mathsf{c}\sigma^{2},\\
\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\mathsf{a}^{*}}{\sqrt{n}}\end{array}\right\}.

Let
\omega(\epsilon):=\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)
. Given
C_{1}>0
, define

\displaystyle r_{n,r,s,\delta}(\mathsf{a}^{*},C_{1}):=L\frac{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\sqrt{n}}+L^{2}\left[\sqrt{\frac{r(d_{1}+d_{2})}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{n}}\right]+\left(1+\frac{1}{C_{1}\sigma L}\right)\mathsf{a}^{*}\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}.

(16)

Theorem 2

(Robust trace regression with additive matrix decomposition)
.

Grant Assumptions
1
-
2
, model (
2
) and assume
\mathbf{X}
is isotropic. Then there are absolute constants
\mathsf{c}\in(0,1)
,
c_{1}\in(0,1/2)
and
C_{0},C_{1}\geq 1
such that the following holds. Suppose
C_{1}^{2}L^{4}\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)\leq c_{1}
. Given

\mathsf{a}^{*}>0
, let
[\hat{\mathbf{B}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
be the solution of (
3
) with
\mathcal{R}:=\|\cdot\|_{N}
,

\mathcal{S}:=\|\cdot\|_{1}
and with tuning parameters
\mathsf{a}:=\mathsf{a}^{*}/\sqrt{n}
,

\lambda\asymp\sigma L^{2}\sqrt{\nicefrac{{d_{1}+d_{2}}}{{n}}},

\chi\asymp\sigma L^{2}\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\log p}}{{n}}}+\nicefrac{{\mathsf{a}^{*}}}{{\sqrt{n}}}

and

\tau\asymp C_{1}L^{2}\sigma/\sqrt{n}
. Assume that

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\gtrsim\left[L^{4}\cdot r(d_{1}+d_{2})\right]\bigvee\left[\left(L^{4}\log p+(\mathsf{a}^{*})^{2}\right)s\right].

(17)

Then, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

\delta\geq\exp\left(-\frac{n}{C_{0}L^{4}}\right),

on an event of probability
\geq 1-\delta
, for all

[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]\in\mathcal{F}(r,s,\mathsf{a}^{*},\mathsf{c})
,

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}\|_{N}+(\nicefrac{{\chi}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\|_{1}+\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\mathbf{B}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{\mathcal{O}(1)}{C_{1}^{2}L^{2}}\right)\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}

(18)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2})r_{n,r,s,\delta}^{2}(\mathsf{a}^{*},C_{1})+\mathcal{O}(C_{1}^{2}\sigma^{2}L^{6})\omega^{2}(\epsilon),

(19)

and also

\displaystyle\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]\|_{\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{O}(1)+\frac{\mathcal{O}(1)}{C_{1}L}\right)\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\sigma)r_{n,r,s,\delta}(\mathsf{a}^{*},C_{1})+\mathcal{O}(C_{1}\sigma L^{3})\omega(\epsilon).

(20)

Theorem
2
states oracle inequalities of the form (
3
).
3
3

3

In case the approximation error is
\mathcal{O}(\sigma)r_{n,r,s,\delta}(\mathsf{a}^{*},1)
— and assuming
L^{2}\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)\leq c_{1}
and
\tau\asymp\sigma L/\sqrt{n}
— we can obtain slightly improved bounds for the estimator in Theorem

. Namely, the error coefficients
\mathcal{O}(1)/C_{1}^{2}L^{2}

\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2})r_{n,r,s,\delta}^{2}(\mathsf{a}^{*},1)
and

respectively. Additionally, the corruption errors
\mathcal{O}(C_{1}^{2}\sigma^{2}L^{6})\omega^{2}(\epsilon)

in (

\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}L^{4})\omega^{2}(\epsilon)
and
\mathcal{O}(\sigma L^{2})\omega(\epsilon)
respectively.

The next proposition ensures the rate in Theorem
2
is optimal up to a log factor.
4
4

4

By the general theory of
[
15
]
, the corruption term
\omega(\epsilon)
is optimal (up to a log term). Thus, it is sufficient to give a lower bound for the non-corrupted model.
Its proof follows from similar arguments in
[
2
]
for the noisy matrix decomposition problem with identity design. Define the class

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(r,s,\mathsf{a}^{*})=\left\{\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{*}:=[\mathbf{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}:\rank(\mathbf{B}^{*})\leq r,\|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}\|_{0}\leq s,\|\mathbf{B}^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\mathsf{a}^{*}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\}.

(21)

For any
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{*}:=[\mathbf{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}
, let
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{*}}
denote the distribution of the data
\{y_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
satisfying (
4
) with parameters

[\mathbf{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}]
. Finally, for some

\sigma>0
, let

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}(r,s,\mathsf{a}^{*}):=\sigma^{2}\left\{\frac{r(d_{1}+d_{2})}{n}+\frac{s}{n}\log\left(\frac{p-s}{s/2}\right)\right\}+(\mathsf{a}^{*})^{2}\frac{s}{n}.

(22)

Proposition 1
.

Assume that
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
are iid
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
independent of
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
,
\mathbf{X}
is isotropic and
\|\mathbf{B}^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathsf{a}^{*}/\sqrt{n}
. Assume
d_{1},d_{2}\geq 10
,
\mathsf{a}^{*}\geq 32\sqrt{\log p}
and
s<p
.

Then there exists universal constants
c>0
and
\beta\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{*}\in\mathcal{A}(r,s,\mathsf{a}^{*})}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{*}}\left\{\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}}]\|_{\Pi}\geq c\Psi_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}(r,s,\mathsf{a}^{*})\right\}\geq\beta,

(23)

where the infimum is taken over all estimators

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=[\hat{\mathbf{B}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
constructed from the data
\{y_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

Next, we state guarantees for the estimator (
3
) with
q=2
for three different parameter classes associated to the subproblems a)-b) in Section
4
. Let
\|\cdot\|
denote either

\|\cdot\|_{0}
or the rank operation

\rank(\cdot)
and
\mathbb{C}_{\mathbf{B}}\subset\mathds{R}^{p}
be a cone parametrized by a point
\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
. Given
d,d_{\eff}\in\mathbb{N}
and

\rho,\mathsf{c},\mathsf{C}>0
, let

\mathcal{F}(d,d_{\eff},\rho,\mathsf{C}):=\{\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}:\|\mathbf{B}\|\leq d,\mathsf{C}L^{2}\rho\mu\left(\mathbb{C}_{\mathbf{B}}\right)\sqrt{\nicefrac{{d_{\eff}}}{{n}}}\leq 1\}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(d,d_{\eff},\rho,\mathsf{c},\mathsf{C}):=\left\{\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{F}(d,d_{\eff},\rho,\mathsf{C}):(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\|\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{B})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\mathsf{c}\sigma^{2}\right\}.

(24)

Consider three cases:

(i)

In sparse regression, take
\mathcal{R}:=\|\cdot\|_{1}
and

\lambda\asymp L^{2}\sigma\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\log p}}{{n}}}.

Set
\|\cdot\|:=\|\cdot\|_{0}
,
\rho:=\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
,
d:=s
,
d_{\eff}:=s\log p
, and
\mathbb{C}_{\boldsymbol{b}}:=\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{b},\|\cdot\|_{1}}(6)
for given
\boldsymbol{b}
— see Section
10
for the definition of this cone.

(ii)

In sparse regression, take

\mathcal{R}:=\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
, the Slope norm in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and

\lambda\asymp L^{2}\sigma\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\sqrt{n}.

Set
\|\cdot\|:=\|\cdot\|_{0}
,
\rho:=\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
,
d:=s
,
d_{\eff}:=s\log(ep/s)
, and the cone

\mathbb{C}_{\boldsymbol{b}}:=\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{s}(6)
for each
\boldsymbol{b}
such that

\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{0}\leq s
— see Section 31

(iii)

In trace regression, take
\mathcal{R}:=\|\cdot\|_{N}
and

\lambda\asymp L^{2}\sigma\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\nicefrac{{d_{1}+d_{2}}}{{n}}}.

Set
\|\cdot\|:=\rank(\cdot)
,
\rho:=\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
,
d:=r
,
d_{\eff}:=r(d_{1}+d_{2})
and the cone

\mathbb{C}_{\mathbf{B}}:=\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\|\cdot\|_{N}}(6)
for given
\mathbf{B}
— see Section
10
.

Let us define

\displaystyle r_{n,d_{\eff},\delta}(\rho,\mu):=L\frac{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\sqrt{n}}+L^{2}\rho\mu\sqrt{\frac{d_{\eff}}{n}}.

(25)

Theorem 3

(Robust sparse/low-rank regression)
.

Grant Assumptions
1
-
2
and model (
2
). Then there are absolute constants
\mathsf{c},c_{1}\in(0,1/2)
and
\mathsf{C},C_{0},C_{1}\geq 1
such that the following holds. Suppose
C_{1}^{2}L^{4}\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)\leq c_{1}
and take
\tau\asymp C_{1}L^{2}\sigma/\sqrt{n}
. Let
\hat{\mathbf{B}}
be the solution of (
3
) with
q=2
— correspondingly to each tuning in cases (i)-(iii). Consider the three different classes of type (
24
) for each of the cases (i)-(iii).

Then, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

\delta\geq\exp\left(-\frac{n}{C_{0}L^{4}}\right),

on an event of probability
\geq 1-\delta
, for all

\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{F}(d,d_{\eff},\rho,\mathsf{c},\mathsf{C})
,

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}})+\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{\mathcal{O}(1)}{C_{1}^{2}L^{2}}\right)\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}

(26)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2})r_{n,d_{\eff},\delta}^{2}(\rho,\mu(\mathbb{C}_{\mathbf{B}}))+\mathcal{O}(C_{1}^{2}\sigma^{2}L^{6})\omega^{2}(\epsilon),

(27)

and also

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}\|_{\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{O}(1)+\frac{\mathcal{O}(1)}{C_{1}L}\right)\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\sigma)r_{n,d_{\eff},\delta}(\rho,\mu(\mathbb{C}_{\mathbf{B}}))+\mathcal{O}(C_{1}\sigma L^{3})\omega(\epsilon).

(28)

From the quadratic process inequality, we may replace
\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
with

\hat{\rho}_{1}:=\max_{j\in[p]}\|\mathbb{X}_{\bullet,j}\|_{2}
and

\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
by its empirical counterpart
\hat{\rho}_{N}
. We now present estimation guarantees for the estimator (

) with
