. Consider three cases:

(i’)

Grant case (i) above but with

\lambda\asymp L\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\log p}}{{n}}}.

(ii’)

Grant case (ii) above but with

\lambda\asymp L\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\sqrt{n}.

(iii’)

Grant case (iii) above but with

\lambda\asymp L\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{\nicefrac{{d_{1}+d_{2}}}{{n}}}.

Theorem 4

(
\sigma
-adaptive robust sparse/low-rank regression)
.

Grant Assumptions
1
-
2
and model (
2
). Then there are absolute constants

c_{1}\in(0,1/2)
and
\mathsf{C},C_{0}\geq 1
such that the following holds. Suppose
L^{2}\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)\leq c_{1}
and take
\tau\asymp L/\sqrt{n}
. Let

\hat{\mathbf{B}}
be the solution of (
3
) with
q=1
— correspondingly to each tuning in cases (i’)-(iii’). Consider the three different classes of type (
24
) for each of the cases (i’)-(iii’).

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

\delta\geq\exp\left(-\frac{n}{C_{0}(L^{4}\vee\sigma^{2})}\right).

Let
\mu_{*}:=\sup_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{F}(d,d_{\eff},\rho,\mathsf{C})}\mu(\mathbb{C}_{\mathbf{B}})
and

\mathsf{c}_{0,n}\asymp r_{n,d_{\eff},\delta}(\rho,\mu_{*})
. Then, on an event of probability
\geq 1-\delta
, it holds that, for all

\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{F}(d,d_{\eff},\rho,\mathsf{c}_{0,n}^{2},\mathsf{C})
,

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}})+\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{c}_{0,n}^{2})\right)\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}

(29)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2})r_{n,d_{\eff},\delta}^{2}(\rho,\mu_{*})+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}L^{4})\omega^{2}(\epsilon),

(30)

and also

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}\|_{\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{O}(1)+\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{c}_{0,n})\right)\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\sigma)r_{n,d_{\eff},\delta}(\rho,\mu_{*})+\mathcal{O}(\sigma L^{2})\omega(\epsilon).

(31)

In all previous theorems, the correspondent estimator is adaptive to
\delta
and the confidence level is
1-\delta
across any
\delta\geq\exp(-cn)
for a fixed constant
c>0
. The estimators are also adaptive to
(s,r,o)
, and, in Theorems
3
-
4
, adaptive to
\mu(\mathbb{C}_{\mathbf{B}})
. In case there is no matrix decomposition, estimator (
3
) with
q=1
achieves, up to constants, the same rate of estimator (
3
) with
q=2
with the advantage of being adaptive to
\sigma
. On the other hand, for
q=1
the approximation must be
\mathcal{O}(\sigma)r_{n,d_{\eff},\delta}(\rho,\mu_{*})
while for
q=2
the approximation error is only required to be
\mathcal{O}(\sigma)
.
5
5

5

In case the approximation error is
\mathcal{O}(\sigma)r_{n,d_{\eff},\delta}(\rho,\mu_{*})
and assuming
L^{2}\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)\leq c_{1}
, the same bounds (
30
)-(
31
) are valid for the estimator (
3
) with
q=2
— with the tuning specified in cases (i)-(iii) and
\tau\asymp\sigma L/\sqrt{n}
.
Nothing is assumed beyond marginal subgaussianity of
(\mathbf{X},\xi)
. In particular, the noise can depend arbitrarily on
\mathbf{X}
, be asymmetric and have zero mass around the origin. From
[
15
]
, the displayed rates are optimal up to the factor
\log(1/\epsilon)
. The approximately linear growth
\epsilon\mapsto\omega(\epsilon)
— in case the sorted Huber loss is used — is confirmed in our numerical experiments (see Section
12
).

Remark 2
.

Within the framework of
M
-estimation with decomposable regularizers, the first obtained oracle inequalities for sparse and trace regression give optimal rates in average
[
10
,
69
]
. Still, as discussed in Section
9
, the rates in these seminal works are suboptimal in
\delta
. Additionally, their tuning assumes knowledge of
\delta
.
[
6
]
was the first work to obtain the subgaussian rate with

\delta
-adaptive estimators for sparse linear regression.
[
31
]
later generalized the bounds of
[
6
]
for the square-root Lasso estimator. Their proof strategy, however, fundamentally assumes the
noise is independent of features
(see Section
9
). When
\epsilon=0
, a corollary of Theorems
3
-
4
is that the same estimators in
[
6
,
31
]
attain the subgaussian rate adaptively to

\delta
assuming only
(\mathbf{X},\xi)
are marginally subgaussian. We refer to Section
9
and Remark
6
in Section
7
for an explanation on these technical issues. See also Section
6.3
. To finish, we remark that when
\epsilon=0
we could prove “sharp” oracle inequalities — that is, with constant
C=1
in (
3
).

6
Related work and contributions

6.1
Robust sparse regression

This model has been the subject of numerous works. From a methodological point of view, the
\ell_{1}
-penalized Huber’s estimator has been considered in
[
79
,
80
,
58
]
. Empirical evaluation for the choice of tuning parameters is comprehensively studied in these papers. For the adversarial model with Gaussian data, fast rates for such estimator have been obtained in
[
12
,
56
,
26
,
25
,
71
]
. The average near optimal rate was shown only recently in
[
27
]
. They obtain the rate

\sqrt{s\log(p/\delta)/n}+\epsilon\log(n/\delta),

with a breakdown point
\epsilon\leq c/\log n
for a constant
c>0
. Under different conditions, the same estimator was later shown in
[
24
]
to attain the subgaussian rate with breakdown point
\epsilon\leq c
without the extra factor
\log(1/\epsilon)
and allowing feature-dependent heavy-tailed noise. These two works are the most closely related to our particular Theorem
3
for robust sparse linear regression.

Remark 3

(Comparison with
[
24
]
)
.

The result in
[
24
]
is interesting: the subgaussian rate is attainable with the standard Huber loss — without the extra
\log(1/\epsilon)
. It also allows feature-dependent heavy-tailed noise — assuming some extra mild conditions. Still, we argue that, in the subgaussian setting, this result is weaker than our Theorem
3
for a sparse parameter. We give two main reasons:

(i)

Even though not explicitly stated, the proof in
[
24
]
is specific to the
oblivious model
— a much weaker model than the adversarial one considered in this work. If
\mathcal{O}
denotes the index set of outliers, they fundamentally use that,
6
6

6

See equation (42) in page 3595 in
[
24
]
.
for all
[\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime}]
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{o}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{O}}(|\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime}\rangle|-\mathbb{E}[|\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime}\rangle|])\right|_{\psi_{2}}\lesssim\frac{\|\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime}\|_{\Pi}}{\sqrt{o}}.

(32)

This follows from Hoeffding’s inequality, but only if
\{\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{O}}
is iid for
fixed

\mathcal{O}
. In the adversarial model,
\mathcal{O}
is an arbitrary random variable dependent on the data set.

(ii)

[
24
]
attains the optimal rate for
\ell_{1}
-regularized Huber regression with penalization

\displaystyle\lambda\asymp\sigma\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\bigvee\mu(\mathbb{C}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}})\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{sn}}\bigvee\mu(\mathbb{C}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}})\frac{o}{\sqrt{s}n}\right),

(33)

and, in case the noise is subgaussian,
\tau\asymp\sigma
. Our tuning
(\lambda,\tau)
follows the very different scaling

\lambda\asymp\sigma\sqrt{\log p/n},

\tau\asymp\sigma/\sqrt{n}.

One notable difference is that our tuning is adaptive to
(s,o,\mu(\mathbb{C}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}}),\delta)
, without resorting to Lepski’s method. If we focus on
(s,o,\mu(\mathbb{C}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}}),\delta)
-adaptive estimators, our guarantees and simulation results are significantly in favor of sorted Huber-type losses instead of the standard Huber loss.

We argue that (i) and the different scaling (
33
) follows from a different proof method. The proof in
[
24
]
is based on
“localization” arguments
for regularized empirical risk minimization (ERM)
[
64
,
57
]
.
7
7

7

This approach has a vast history. State-of-the art results were given in the seminal paper
[
64
]
— introducing the “small-ball method” for ERM with the square loss. With it, proper localized control of the quadratic and multiplier processes entail optimal rates.
[
57
]
generalized this method to analyze regularized ERM. A key tool in this work is the so called “sparsity equation”. This elegant method entails, in particular, optimality of Lasso, Slope and trace regression.
Being more precise,
[
24
]
is able to show that regularized ERM with convex Lipschitz losses
[
3
,
23
]
is robust against contaminated labels
8
8

8

The works
[
3
,
23
]
also use the “sparsity equation” but, unlike
[
64
,
57
]
, do not use explicit concentration of the quadratic/multiplier processes. For convex Lipschitz losses satisfying the “Bernstein condition”, localized concentration of the empirical process suffices.
, assuming the
oblivious
model. In this approach, one uses the fact that the loss based on Huber’s function satisfies the so called ‘‘Bernstein’s condition’’ --- under additional mild noise conditions.
9
9

9

[
24
]
uses Theorem 7 in
[
23
]
stating that, under subgaussian designs and noises with positive mass around the origin, the so called “Bernstein’s condition” is satisfied by most convex Lipschitz losses of interest. This additional noise condition is not a serious restriction in many settings — it also allows heavy-tailed noise. Still, it is unnecessary in the subgaussian setting — giving some additional evidence that both proof methods are different.
Our proof method does not follow the “localization” literature but rather the literature on
M
-estimation with decomposable regularizers

[
10
,
69
,
6
]
. In this approach, we do not use Lipschitz continuity of Huber-type losses. In fact, our analysis uses a loss based on the square cost and defined over an augmented variable — see (
3
)-(
3
).

Remark 4

(Comparison with
[
27
]
)
.

Granting reasons (i)-(ii) in Remark
3
,
[
27
]
is the closest work to ours — indeed, they consider the adversarial model and
(s,o,\mu(\mathbb{C}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}}))
-adaptive estimators. Our most noted improvements in terms of rate guarantees are three-fold. First, we show that sorted Huber-type regression has improved bounds compared to Huber-regression: the corruption error
\epsilon\log n
and breakdown point
c/\log n
of the latter is replaced by
\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)
and breakdown point
c
. We give numerical evidence of the superiority of sorted Huber regression compared to standard Huber regression (see Figure
1
). Adaptations of Huber regression have been studied before. Still, they usually involve modifying the scaling of the tuning parameter. To our knowledge, our theoretical and empirical results for sorted Huber-type losses give new insights. Second, unlike the results in
[
27
]
, our rates for sparse regression use
\delta
-adaptive estimators attaining the optimal
\delta
-subgaussian rate under weaker assumptions — namely, subgaussian feature-dependent noise. See Remark
2
, Section
6.3
and pointers therein. Thirdly, we give optimal guarantees for robust sparse regression with
\sigma
-adaptive estimators under the same set of assumptions. In Section
11
, we explain that the proof of Theorem
4
is an adaptation of the proof of Theorem
3
.

To finish, we remark that our improvements on
[
27
]
are substantial in terms of proof techniques and structural properties. In fact, our main focus is the broader model RTRMD. See the next Section
6.3
and pointers therein.

Remark 5

(Further references in robust sparse regression)
.

We complement our review mentioning some literature analyzing different contamination models, e.g. dense bounded noise
[
88
,
59
,
70
,
42
,
1
]
. This setting is also studied in
[
51
]
with the LAD-estimator
[
87
]
. Alternatively, a refined analysis of iterative thresholding methods were considered in
[
9
,
8
,
82
,
67
]
. They obtain sharp breakdown points and consistency bounds for the oblivious model. Works on sparse linear regression with covariate contamination were considered early on by
[
18
]
and, more recently, in
[
4
]
, albeit with worst rates and breakdown points compared to the response contamination model. Works by
[
60
,
61
]
have also studied the optimality of sparse linear regression in models with error-in-variables and missing-data covariates. Although out of scope, we mention for completeness that tractable algorithms for linear regression with covariate contamination have been intensively investigated in the low-dimensional scaling (
n\geq p
), with initial works by
[
34
,
36
,
75
]
and more recent ones in
[
29
,
22
,
73
,
72
]
.

6.2
Robust trace regression

The first bounds on trace regression (with no corruption) were presented, e.g., in
[
68
,
78
,
69
,
90
]
following the framework of
M
-estimation with decomposable regularizers.
10
10

10

The complementary works
[
57
,
3
,
23
]
also study trace regression with different techniques. See Remark
3
.
Trace regression with label-feature contamination is studied in detail in
[
44
]
. This paper is based on Tukey’s depth, a hard computational problem in high dimensions. The recent papers
[
41
,
81
]
focus on models with heavy-tailed noise. The interesting paper
[
81
]
considers label contamination as well, but follows a methodology based on non-convex optimization, presenting bounds for a gradient-descent method. As such, it is hard to compare their results with ours, as they follow different set of assumptions. For instance, they assume the oblivious model --- a more restrictive model than the adversarial one. Other minor differences include the assumptions that the covariance matrix is invertible, the noise has positive density around the origin
11
11

11

They use similar assumptions as in
[
40
,
3
,
23
,
24
]
.
and that some conditions are satisfied to ensure good initialization.

Robust trace regression is not considered in
[
24
,
27
]
. Still, their methods could be applied to this problem. In that case, the exact same comments in Remarks
3
-
4
would still apply — changing
(s,\log p)
by
(r,d_{1}+d_{2})
. As mentioned in Section
6.1
, our improvements on guarantees and assumptions are in fact consequences of new proof techniques and structural design properties motivated to study the broader model RTRMD. We discuss this point in the next section.

6.3
Robust trace regression with additive matrix decomposition

The statistical theory for this model is the main concern of this work. In fact, the results in Sections
6.1
-
6.2
are consequences of the techniques needed to analyze the broader model RTRMD. As mentioned in the introduction, additive matrix decomposition was extensively studied in
[
89
,
14
,
13
,
91
,
48
,
63
,
2
]
. Still, there is currently no optimality theory for additive matrix decomposition in trace regression — nor its extension with label contamination.
In this preliminary section, we briefly comment on three design properties needed to establish an optimality theory for RTRMD. A detailed discussion is referred to later sections.

When the parameter is the sum of a low-rank and sparse matrices, the random design in trace regression is singular with high probability. We identify a concentration inequality for the product process (see our Theorem
7
) as the sufficient property to prove restricted strong convexity for this model. In Definition
8
in Section
8
, we denote this property by
\PP
. To the best of our knowledge, this is a novel application of product processes in high-dimensional statistics. This technical property is “sharp”, in the sense that replacing it with other naive methods, e.g. dual-norm inequalities, fail to entail the optimal rate.

\PP
is no longer sufficient in case of label contamination. To handle it, we take inspiration from
[
27
]
. This work identified one design property sufficient to handle label contamination when the parameter is sparse. Termed “incoherence principle” (
\IP
), it is derived from Chevet’s inequality.
[
27
]
is not concerned with matrix decomposition, and as such,
\PP
is unnecessary. We show that a generalized version of
\IP
(see Definition
8
in Section
8
) and the new property
\PP
are
jointly
sufficient properties to ensure restricted strong convexity for RTRMD and to optimaly control the “design-corruption interaction”. Again,
\PP
and
\IP
are “sharp”: mere use of dual-norm inequalities fail to achieve optimality. See Remarks
7
-
8
in Section
8
and Remarks
9
-
11
in Section
10
.

The third design property we use enables us to achieve the optimal
\delta
-subgaussian rate with
\delta
-adaptive estimators, even when the noise is feature-dependent. This property, denoted by
\MP
in Definition
8
in Section
8
, follows from a concentration inequality for the multiplier process (see Theorem
6
in Section
7
). In the framework of
M
-estimation with decomposable regularizers,
\MP
is a classical property used to control the “design-noise interaction”
[
10
,
2
,
6
,
27
]
. The typical way to prove it is via the dual-norm inequality
[
10
,
2
,
27
]
. This approach fails to entail the subgaussian rate and
\delta
-adaptivity.
[
6
]
was the first to succeed on this point, using a suitable version of

\MP
(see Remark
2
). Still, they assume feature-independent noise — in case the parameter is sparse and there is no contamination. Our version of
\MP
in Definition
8
in Section
8
is more general than
[
6
]
so to handle feature-dependent noise, additive matrix decomposition and label contamination.

To finish, as discussed in Section
6.1
, the relevance of sorted Huber-type losses also applies to RTRMD. Using the standard Huber’s loss, we would have rate
\omega(\epsilon)\asymp\epsilon\log n
instead of
\omega(\epsilon)\asymp\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)
, breakdown point
\epsilon\leq c/\log n
instead of
c
, for some constant
c\in(0,1/2)
. See also Figure
5
(b) in Section
12
.

7
The multiplier and product processes

In this section we present concentration inequalities for subgaussian Multiplier and Product processes with optimal dependence on
(d_{\eff},\delta)
. The notation in this section is independent of all previous sections. Throughout this section,
(B,\mathcal{B},\mathbf{P})
is a probability space,
(\xi,X)
is a random (possibly not independent) pair taking values
on
\mathbb{R}\times B
and
X
has marginal distribution
\mathbf{P}
.
\{(\xi_{i},X_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
will denote an iid copy of
(\xi,X)
and
\hat{\mathbf{P}}
be denotes the empirical measure associated to
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. The
multiplier process
over functions
f\in F
is defined as

M(f):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}(\xi_{i}f(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\xi f(X)]).

For instance, the
empirical process
is a particular case when

\xi\equiv 1
. The
product process
is defined as

A(f,g):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\bigg\{f(X_{i})g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}f(X_{i})g(X_{i})\bigg\},

over two distinct classes
F
and
G
of measurable functions. When
F=G
, the correspondent process is often termed the
quadratic process
.

There is a large literature on concentration of these processes and its use in risk minimization. One pioneering idea is of “generic chaining”, first developed by Talagrand for the empirical process
[
83
]
. This method was refined by Dirksen, Bednorz, Mendelson and collaborators, e.g., in
[
66
,
37
,
5
,
65
]
. The following notion of complexity is used in generic chaining bounds.
12
12

12

The pioneering work by Talagrand presented the, somewhat mysterious,
\gamma_{2}
-functional as a measure of complexity of the class. The “truncated”

\gamma_{2,p}
-functional was presented recently by Dirksen
[
37
]
.

Definition 5

(
\gamma_{2,p}
-functional)
.

Let
(T,\mathsf{d})
be a pseudo-metric space. We say a sequence
(T_{k})
of subsets of
T
is
admissible
if
|T_{0}|=1
and
|T_{k}|\leq 2^{2^{k}}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\cup_{k\geq 0}T_{k}
is dense in
T
. Let
\mathcal{A}
denote the class of all such admissible subset sequences. Given
p\geq 1
, the

\gamma_{2,p}
-functional with respect to
(T,\mathsf{d})
is the quantity

\displaystyle\gamma_{2,p}(T):=\inf_{(T_{k})\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{t\in T}\sum_{k\geq\lfloor\log_{2}p\rfloor}2^{k/2}\mathsf{d}(t,T_{k}).

(34)

We will say that
(T_{k})\in\mathcal{A}
is optimal if it achieves the infimum above. Set
\gamma_{2}(T):=\gamma_{2,1}(T)
.

Let
L_{\psi_{2}}=L_{\psi_{2}}(\mathbf{P})
be the family of measurable functions
f:B\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
having finite
\psi_{2}
-norm

\|f\|_{\psi_{2}}:=|f(X)|_{\psi_{2}}:=\inf\{c>0:\mathbb{E}[\psi_{2}(\nicefrac{{f(X)}}{{c}})]\leq 1\}

where
\psi_{2}(t):=e^{t^{2}}-1
. We assume that the
\psi_{2}
-norm of
\xi
, denoted also by

\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}
, is finite. Given
f,g\in L_{\psi_{2}}
, we define the pseudo-distance

\mathsf{d}(f,g):=\|f-g\|_{\psi_{2}}.

Given a subclass
F\subset L_{\psi_{2}}
, we let
\Delta(F):=\sup_{f,f^{\prime}\in F}\mathsf{d}(f,f^{\prime})
and
\bar{\Delta}(F):=\sup_{f\in F}\mathsf{d}(f,0)
. We prove the following two results in Sections B
and C

Theorem 6

(Multiplier process)
.

There exists universal constant
c>0
, such that for all
f_{0}\in F
,
n\geq 1
,
u\geq 1
and
v\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-ce^{-u/4}-ce^{-nv}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in F}|M(f)-M(f_{0})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sqrt{v}+1\right)\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\frac{\gamma_{2}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}+\left(\sqrt{\frac{2u}{n}}+\frac{u}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{uv}{n}}\right)\|\xi\|_{\psi_{2}}\bar{\Delta}(F).

(35)

Theorem 7

(Product process)
.

Let
F,G
be subclasses of
L_{\psi_{2}}
. There exist universal constants
c,C>0
, such that for all
n\geq 1
and
u\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-e^{-u}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}\left|A(f,g)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left[\frac{\gamma_{2}(F)\gamma_{2}(G)}{n}+\bar{\Delta}(F)\frac{\gamma_{2}(G)}{\sqrt{n}}+\bar{\Delta}(G)\frac{\gamma_{2}(F)}{\sqrt{n}}\right]

(36)

\displaystyle+c\sup_{(f,g)\in F\times G}\|fg-\mathbf{P}fg\|_{\psi_{1}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{u}{n}}+\frac{u}{n}\right).

(37)

Remark 6

(Confidence level & complexity)
.

Mendelson
[
65
]
established impressive concentration inequalities for the multiplier and product processes. In fact, they hold for much more general
(\xi,X)
having heavier tails (see Theorems 1.9, 1.13 and 4.4 in
[
65
]
). When specifying these bounds to subgaussian classes and noise, however, the confidence parameter
u>0
multiplies the complexities
\gamma_{2}(F)
and

\gamma_{2}(G)
— unlike our Theorems
6
-
7
.
For a related discussion regarding the empirical and quadratic processes, we refer to Remark 3.3(ii) and observations before Corollary 5.7 in Dirksen’s paper
[
37
]
. This technical point is crucial in our proof to show that our class of estimators attain the
\delta
-subgaussian rate in the high-dimensional regime with
\delta
-adaptive estimators. We refer to Section
9
for a discussion on this topic. Note that we can take
v\asymp 1
for failure probability
\delta\geq e^{-c^{\prime}n}
for absolute constant
c^{\prime}>0
. Our proofs are motivated by Dirksen’s method for the quadratic process
[
37
]
and Talagrand’s proof for the empirical process
[
83
]
.
13
13

13

They are not corollaries of Dirksen’s results. For instance, Theorem
7
cannot be derived from the quadratic process inequality and the parallelogram law. Indeed, we fundamentally need
F\neq Q
.

8
Properties for subgaussian distributions

In what follows,

\mathfrak{M}(\mathbf{V},\boldsymbol{u}):=\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{V})+\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{u},

\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
are norms on
\mathds{R}^{p}
and
\mathcal{Q}
is a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Throughout this section,
(\mathbf{X},\xi)\in\mathds{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}
satisfies Assumption
2
. Next, we define the empirical bilinear form

\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{V}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=\langle\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V}),\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{W})\rangle.

In what follows,
\{\mathsf{a}_{i},\mathsf{b}_{i},\mathsf{c}_{i},\mathsf{d}_{i},\mathsf{f}_{i}\}
are fixed positive numbers.

Definition 8
.

(i)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies

\TP_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2})
if for all
\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
,

\displaystyle\big\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V})\big\|_{2}\geq\mathsf{a}_{1}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\Pi}-\mathsf{a}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V}).

(38)

(ii)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies
\PP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{c}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{4})
if for all
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{n}-\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{c}_{1}\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\Pi}\|\mathbf{W}\|_{\Pi}+\mathsf{c}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\|\mathbf{W}\|_{\Pi}+\mathsf{c}_{3}\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\Pi}\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{W})

(39)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{c}_{4}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{W}).

(40)

(iii)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies

\IP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{4})
if for all
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle|\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{W})\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{b}_{1}\left\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}]\right\|_{\Pi}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{3}\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{W})\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}

(41)

\displaystyle+\mathsf{b}_{4}\left\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}]\right\|_{\Pi}\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u}).

(42)

(iv)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies
\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{3},\mathsf{d}_{4})
if for all

[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\left\{\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u})\|_{2}^{2}-2\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}\right\}_{+}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{d}_{1}\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}-\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})-\mathsf{d}_{3}\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{W})-\mathsf{d}_{4}\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u}).

(43)

(v)

(\mathfrak{X},\boldsymbol{\xi})
satisfies
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\mathsf{f}_{3},\mathsf{f}_{4})
if
for all
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle|\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u})\rangle|\leq\mathsf{f}_{1}\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}+\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})+\mathsf{f}_{3}\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{W})+\mathsf{f}_{4}\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{u}).

(44)

In the next lemmas, we show that
\TP
and
\ATP
are consequences of
\PP
and
\IP
.

Lemma 9
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{X}
satisfies
\PP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4})
with
\alpha_{1}\in(0,1)
. Then
\TP_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2})
holds with constants

\mathsf{a}_{1}:=\sqrt{(3/4)(1-\alpha_{1})}

and

\mathsf{a}_{2}:=\left\{\frac{(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3})^{2}}{(1-\alpha_{1})}+\alpha_{4}\right\}^{1/2}.

Lemma 10

(Lemma 7 in
[
27
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{X}
satisfies
\TP_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2})
and
\IP_{\mathcal{R},0,\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},0,\mathsf{b}_{4})
. Suppose further that
\mathsf{a}_{1}\in(0,1)
and

\mathsf{b}_{1}<3\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}/4.

Then
\ATP_{\mathcal{R},0,\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},0,\mathsf{d}_{4})
holds with constants

\mathsf{d}_{1}:=\sqrt{(\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})\mathsf{a}_{1}^{2}-\mathsf{b}_{1}}
,

\mathsf{d}_{2}:=\frac{2\mathsf{b}_{2}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}+\mathsf{a}_{2},

and
\mathsf{d}_{4}:=\frac{2\mathsf{b}_{4}}{\mathsf{a}_{1}}
.

Lemma 11
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{X}
satisfies
\TP_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2})
,
\TP_{\mathcal{S}}(\bar{\mathsf{a}}_{1},\bar{\mathsf{a}}_{2})
,
\IP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{4})
and

\PP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{c}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{4})
with
\mathsf{c}_{4}=\gamma\mathsf{c}_{2}\mathsf{c}_{3}
for some
\gamma>0
. Suppose further that
\mathsf{a}_{1},\bar{\mathsf{a}}_{1}\in(0,1)
and

\mathsf{b}_{1}+\mathsf{c}_{1}<3(\mathsf{a}_{1}\wedge\bar{\mathsf{a}}_{1})^{2}/4.

Then
\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{3},\mathsf{d}_{4})
holds with constants
\mathsf{d}_{1}:=\sqrt{(\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}})(\mathsf{a}_{1}\wedge\bar{\mathsf{a}}_{1})^{2}-(\mathsf{b}_{1}+\mathsf{c}_{1})}
and

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{2}:=\left\{\frac{8(\mathsf{b}_{2}^{2}+\mathsf{c}_{2}^{2})}{(\mathsf{a}_{1}\wedge\bar{\mathsf{a}}_{1})^{2}}+\gamma\mathsf{c}_{2}^{2}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathsf{a}_{2},\quad\mathsf{d}_{3}:=\left\{\frac{8(\mathsf{b}_{3}^{2}+\mathsf{c}_{3}^{2})}{(\mathsf{a}_{1}\wedge\bar{\mathsf{a}}_{1})^{2}}+\gamma\mathsf{c}_{3}^{2}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\bar{\mathsf{a}}_{2},\quad\mathsf{d}_{4}:=\frac{2\sqrt{2}\mathsf{b}_{4}}{(\mathsf{a}_{1}\wedge\bar{\mathsf{a}}_{1})}.

(45)

In the following sections, we will not explicitly use
\PP
. From the previous lemmas, it should be clear that this property is implicitly used when we invoke
\TP
and
\ATP
. The fundamental properties we need to show for subgaussian designs and noises are
\PP
,
\IP
and

\MP
. The next proposition ensures these properties hold with high probability.

Proposition 2
.

Grant Assumption
2
. There is universal constant
C>0
such that the following holds. Let

\delta\in(0,1)
.

(i)

With probability
\geq 1-\delta
,
\PP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{c}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{4})
is satisfied with constants

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{1}

\displaystyle=CL^{2}\left(\frac{1+\log(1/\delta)}{n}+\frac{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

(46)

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{2}

\displaystyle=CL^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\mathscr{G}\left(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\right),

(47)

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{3}

\displaystyle=CL^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\mathscr{G}\left(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{S}})\right),

(48)

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{CL^{2}}{n}\mathscr{G}\left(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\right)\cdot\mathscr{G}\left(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{S}})\right).

(49)

(ii)

Suppose that

\mathsf{c}(\delta):=CL^{2}(\frac{1+\log(1/\delta)}{n}+\frac{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\sqrt{n}})<1
. Then, with probability
\geq 1-\delta
,

\TP_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2})
holds with constants

\mathsf{a}_{1}=\sqrt{(3/4)(1-\mathsf{c}(\delta))}

and

\mathsf{a}_{2}=\left(\frac{2CL^{2}}{\sqrt{1-\mathsf{c}(\delta)}}\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)+\frac{CL}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\mathscr{G}\left(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}})\right).

(iii)

With probability
\geq 1-\delta
,

\IP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{4})
holds with constants

\mathsf{b}_{1}=CL\frac{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\sqrt{n}},

\mathsf{b}_{2}=CL\frac{\mathscr{G}(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}}))}{\sqrt{n}},

\mathsf{b}_{3}=CL\frac{\mathscr{G}(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{S}}))}{\sqrt{n}}

and

\mathsf{b}_{4}=CL\frac{\mathscr{G}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}})}{\sqrt{n}}.

(iv)

Define the quantities

\displaystyle\triangle_{n}(\delta)

\displaystyle:=(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})[1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}]+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})[1+\log(1/\delta)+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}],

(50)

\displaystyle\lozenge_{n}(\delta)

\displaystyle:=(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})[1+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}]+(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}).

(51)

With probability
\geq 1-\delta
,
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\mathsf{f}_{3},\mathsf{f}_{4})
holds with constants
\mathsf{f}_{1}:=C\sigma L\triangle_{n}(\delta)
,
\mathsf{f}_{2}:=C\sigma L\lozenge_{n}(\delta)\cdot\mathscr{G}(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{R}}))
,
\mathsf{f}_{3}:=C\sigma L\lozenge_{n}(\delta)\cdot\mathscr{G}(\mathfrak{S}^{1/2}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{S}}))
and
\mathsf{f}_{4}:=\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\mathscr{G}\left(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}\right)
.

Proof sketch.

\PP
follows from the concentration inequality for the product process given in Theorem
7
and a two-parameter peeling argument.
\TP
follows from
\PP
and Lemma
9
.
14
14

14

Alternatively,
\TP
could be proved from Dirksen-Bednorz inequality for the quadratic process
[
37
,
5
]
and a one-parameter peeling lemma.

To prove
\IP
, we invoke Chevet’s inequality for subgaussian processes twice — for each of the pair of norms
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q})
and
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q})
— and a two-parameter peeling lemma. To prove

\MP
, we invoke the multiplier process inequality of Theorem
6
twice — for each of the norms
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q})
. We also concentrate the linear process

\boldsymbol{u}\mapsto\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\xi}\rangle
using a symmetrization-comparison argument with a Gaussian linear process. From these three bounds and a one-parameter peeling lemma,
\MP
follows. The proofs of these claims are referred to Sections
16
,
17
,
18
and
19
of the supplement.
∎

\TP
is the well known “restricted strong convexity” used to analyze regularized
M
-estimators with decomposable norms
[
10
,
61
,
69
,
6
]
.
\MP
over a single variable
\mathbf{V}
and norm

\mathcal{R}
is also well known to be useful. We generalize this concept over the triple
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]
and norms
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q})
. See the next Section
9
for a discussion on this notion. Similarly,
\IP
,
\PP
and
\ATP
— an abbreviation for “augmented” restricted convexity — are properties over the triplet
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]
which we show to be useful for RTRMD.

Remark 7

(Design properties in
[
2
]
)
.

The main design property in
[
2
]
is stated in their Definition 2. In our terminology, it is equivalent to
\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},0}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{3},0)
, a particular version over the pair
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}
. Their Theorem 1 states deterministic bounds assuming this property. Still, they guarantee this property holds only for two simple cases. The first is identity designs, for which
\ATP
is trivially satisfied with
\mathsf{d}_{1}=1
and
\mathsf{d}_{2}=\mathsf{d}_{3}=0
. The second case is multi-task learning. Dirksen’s inequality for the quadratic process implies the design is invertible with high probability.
15
15

15

The design components are
\mathfrak{X}_{i}(\mathbf{B}):=\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}
. When
n\gtrsim d_{1}
, standard concentration inequalities imply

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{b})^{2}\geq c\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{\Pi}^{2}

for all
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
with high probability for some absolute constant
c\in(0,1)
. Thus,

\frac{1}{n}\|\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{B})\|_{F}^{2}\geq c\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\Pi}^{2}

for all
\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
.
Even without label contamination, proving

\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},0}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{3},0)
for trace regression with additive matrix decomposition requires a more refined argument using
\PP
.

The model in
[
2
]
is not concerned with label contamination. Using our terminology, they do not need
\IP

(
\mathsf{b}_{i}\equiv 0
) and it is enough to set
\mathcal{Q}\equiv 0
,

\mathsf{c}_{4}=\mathsf{d}_{4}=\mathsf{f}_{4}=0
. Additionally, they implicitly use
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},0}(0,\mathsf{f}_{2},\mathsf{f}_{3},0)
with

\mathsf{f}_{1}=0
, resorting to the dual-norm inequality. As explained in the next section, this is the technical reason their bounds are optimal in average but sub-optimal in

\delta
. We remind that they assume the noise is independent of the features.

Remark 8

(Design properties in
[
27
]
)
.

[
27
]
studies robust sparse regression with Huber’s loss in the Gaussian setting. In this quest, they require the particular properties
\IP_{\|\cdot\|_{1},0,\|\cdot\|_{1}}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},0,\mathsf{b}_{4})
and
\ATP_{\|\cdot\|_{1},0,\|\cdot\|_{1}}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},0,\mathsf{d}_{4})
over the pair
[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
16
16

16

They use the notation TP for
\TP
and ATP for
\ATP
.
Explicitly:

\displaystyle|\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{v})\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{b}_{1}\left\|\boldsymbol{v}\right\|_{\Pi}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{1}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{4}\left\|\boldsymbol{v}\right\|_{\Pi}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{1},

(52)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{d}_{1}\|[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}-\mathsf{d}_{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{1}-\mathsf{d}_{4}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{1}.

(53)

Their model is not concerned with matrix decomposition. Using our terminology, they do not need
\PP
(
\mathsf{c}_{i}\equiv 0
) and it is enough to set
\mathcal{S}\equiv 0
,

\mathsf{b}_{3}=\mathsf{d}_{3}=\mathsf{f}_{3}=0
. Our framework deals with the broader model RTRMD. By Lemma
11
and Proposition
2
, RTRMD requires a non trivial interplay between the product process inequality of Theorem
7

and Chevet’s inequality (see Section
18
in the supplement). Both inequalities are needed as they imply, respectively,
\PP
and the general version of

\IP
over the triple
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and norms
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q})
.

We now justify the relevance of regressing with sorted Huber-type losses. In properties
\IP
and
\MP
in Proposition
2
,

\mathsf{b}_{4}\asymp\mathsf{f}_{4}\asymp\sigma\mathscr{G}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}})/\sqrt{n}
. In Huber regression
(
\mathcal{Q}=\|\cdot\|_{1}
), one has

\mathsf{b}_{4}\asymp\mathsf{f}_{4}\asymp\sigma\{\log n/n\}^{1/2}
. With sorted Huber-type losses
(
\mathcal{Q}=\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
),

\mathsf{b}_{4}\asymp\mathsf{f}_{4}\asymp\sigma/\sqrt{n}
. Using this observation in Theorem
15
, we obtain the improvement on the corruption rate
\omega(\epsilon)
and breakdown point by a factor
\log n
. For the details, see the proof of Theorem
2
in Section 28
in the supplement.
Our simulations also show an improvement on the “practical” constant in the MSE (recall Figure
1
).

To conclude, the analysis in
[
27
]
implicitly uses the particular version
\MP_{\mathcal{R},0,\mathcal{Q}}(0,\mathsf{f}_{2},0,\mathsf{f}_{4})
with
\mathsf{f}_{1}=0
— indeed, they resort to the dual-norm inequality. As in the previous remark, this approach leads to near-optimal bounds in
(n,s,p,\epsilon)
in average but sub-optimal in
\delta
. We prove the general version of
\MP
for RTRMD is defined over the triple
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and norms
(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp})
.
[
27
]
also assumes the noise is independent of the features.

9
\MP
in
M
-estimation with decomposable regularizers

Let
\hat{\mathbf{B}}
be the least-squares estimator with penalization

\lambda\mathcal{R}
— when there is no contamination,
the model is well-specified and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}\equiv\mathbf{0}
. By the first-order condition, one gets

\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}})\|_{2}^{2}\leq(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\mathfrak{X}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}})\rangle+\lambda\big(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{*})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\big).

In the “Lasso proof”, the standard argument is to properly upper bound the RHS and lower bound the LHS — using the regularization effect of the decomposable norm

\mathcal{R}
. For the upper bound, the typical way is to use the dual-norm inequality:

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\mathfrak{X}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}})\rangle\leq(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\mathcal{R}^{*}(\mathfrak{X}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\xi}))\cdot\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}),

(54)

where
\mathcal{R}^{*}
denotes the dual-norm of

\mathcal{R}
and
\mathfrak{X}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\xi}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\mathbf{X}_{i}
is the adjoint operator of

\mathfrak{X}
. The displayed bound is

\MP_{\mathcal{R},0,0}(0,\mathsf{f}_{2},0,0)
with

\mathsf{f}_{2}=(1/n)\mathcal{R}^{*}(\mathfrak{X}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\xi}))
. As well known, the choice
\lambda\gtrsim(1/n)\mathcal{R}^{*}(\mathfrak{X}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\xi}))
ensures

\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}}
lies in a dimension-reduction cone.
\TP
can then be invoked for the lower bound — indeed, it implies strong-convexity over the dimension-reduction cone.

Consider trace regression with

\mathcal{R}=\|\cdot\|_{N}
. As first shown in
[
68
]
, if we use (
54
) we must take

\lambda\asymp\sigma\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})(\sqrt{(d_{1}+d_{2})/n}+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n})
, implying the estimation rate

\sigma\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{r(d_{1}+d_{2})/n}+\sigma\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{r\log(1/\delta)/n}.

This seminal result is optimal — in average but subptimal in

\delta
. In case of sparse regression, the same approach leads to the rate

\sigma\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{s\log p/n}+\sigma\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sqrt{s\log(1/\delta)/n}.

To our knowledge,
[
6
]
was the first to attain the
\delta
-subgaussian rate for sparse regression. See also
[
31
]
for extensions on their results for the square-root Lasso and Slope estimators. Let
\mathbb{X}
denote the design matrix satisfying

\max_{j\in[p]}\|\mathbb{X}_{\bullet,j}\|_{2}\leq 1
and assume that
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is independent of
\mathbb{X}
. In their Theorem 9.1, they show that, with probability
\geq 1-\delta
, for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\mathbb{X}\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\leq\tilde{\mathsf{f}}_{1}\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}+\tilde{\mathsf{f}}_{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\sharp},

(55)

with
\tilde{\mathsf{f}}_{1}\asymp\sigma(\nicefrac{{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
and
\tilde{\mathsf{f}}_{2}\asymp\sigma/\sqrt{n}
. The above bound and an upper bound on the quadratic process imply

\MP_{\|\cdot\|_{\sharp},0,0}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},0,0)
with

\mathsf{f}_{1}\asymp\tilde{\mathsf{f}}_{1}
and
\mathsf{f}_{2}\asymp\tilde{\mathsf{f}}_{2}
.
17
17

17

Dirksen’s inequality implies, for suitable constants
\tilde{\mathsf{a}}_{1},\tilde{\mathsf{a}}_{2}>0
,
\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq\tilde{\mathsf{a}}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\Pi}+\tilde{\mathsf{a}}_{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\sharp}
for all
\boldsymbol{v}
with high probability.
It is a substantial improvement upon (
54
) in the sense that
\tilde{\mathsf{f}}_{2}
does not depend on
\delta
— appearing only in the “variance” constant
\tilde{\mathsf{f}}_{1}
. This allows us to take

\lambda\asymp\sigma/\sqrt{n}
, entailing the subgaussian rate

\sigma\sqrt{s\log(p/s)/n}+\sigma\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n}.

In conclusion, showing
\MP_{\mathcal{R},0,0}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},0,0)
with finer arguments than (
54
) — with proper coefficient
\mathsf{f}_{1}\neq 0
— is the technical reason
[
6
]
attains the
\delta
-subgaussian rate with
\delta
-adaptive estimators in the framework of
M
-estimation with decomposable regularizers.

Theorem 9.1 in
[
6
]
fundamentally assumes

\boldsymbol{\xi}
is independent of
\mathbf{X}
.
18
18

18

Indeed, define the random norm

\hat{T}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\frac{\|\mathbb{X}^{(n)}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}}{L}\vee\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\sharp}
with
L:=\sqrt{n/\log(1/\delta)}/\sigma
. In case
\mathbb{X}
is fixed, they bound the multiplier process concentrating the linear process

\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{B}_{\hat{T}}}\langle\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{u}\rangle

— see Proposition 9.2 in
[
6
]
. The proof of this elegant result follows from a simple application of a tail symmetrization-comparison argument and the gaussian concentration inequality. Peeling is not necessary — as homogeneity of norms suffices.

Without this assumption, Theorem
6
and a peeling argument entail

\MP_{\|\cdot\|_{\sharp},0,0}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},0,0)
with high probability and constants
\mathsf{f}_{1}\asymp\sigma L(\nicefrac{{1+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
and
\mathsf{f}_{2}\asymp\sigma L\rho_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\sqrt{n}
— assuming
n\gtrsim 1+\log(1/\delta)
. More generally, we prove
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\mathsf{f}_{3},\mathsf{f}_{4})
with non-zero constants
\mathsf{f}_{3}
and
\mathsf{f}_{4}\asymp\sigma L\mathscr{G}(\mathbb{B}_{\mathcal{Q}})/\sqrt{n}
for general regularization norms

(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q})
. When

\mathcal{Q}=\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}
, we show this property is useful to handle label contamination and/or additive matrix decomposition. These properties entail the
\delta
-subgaussian rate for the robust
\delta
-adaptive estimators (
3
)-(
3
) — assuming just marginal subgaussianity of
\mathbf{X}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}
.

10
First general theorem

The main result of this section is the general Theorem
15
. To stated it, we fix the positive constants
\{\mathsf{a}_{i}\}
,
\{\mathsf{b}_{i}\}
,
\{\mathsf{c}_{i}\}
,
\{\mathsf{d}_{i}\}
and
\{\mathsf{f}_{i}\}
in Definition
8
. Theorems
2
-
3
are consequences of Theorem
15
and Proposition
2
— which ensure that the required design properties hold with high probability.

We start with some definitions.

Definition 12

(Decomposable norms
[
69
,
53
]
)
.

A norm
\mathcal{R}
over
\mathds{R}^{p}
is said to be decomposable if, for all
\mathbf{B}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
, there exists linear map
\mathbf{V}\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{V})
such that, for all
\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
, defining

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}):=\mathbf{V}-\mathcal{P}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V})
,

•

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{B})=0
,

•

\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}),\mathcal{P}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V})\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle=0
,

•

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}))+\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}))
.

When
\mathcal{R}=\|\cdot\|_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
is the projection onto the support of vector
\boldsymbol{b}
. When
\mathcal{R}=\|\cdot\|_{N}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}
is the projection onto the “low-rank” support of the matrix
\mathbf{B}
— see Section
20
in the supplement for a precise definition. In what follows,
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
will be decomposable norms on
\mathds{R}^{p}
. We shall need the following definition.

Definition 13

(Dimension-reduction cone)
.

Given
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}
, let

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
be the projection maps associated to
(\mathcal{R},\mathbf{B})
and
(\mathcal{S},\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
respectively. Fix
c_{0},\gamma_{\mathcal{R}},\gamma_{\mathcal{S}},\eta\geq 0
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}}(c_{0},\gamma_{\mathcal{R}},\gamma_{\mathcal{S}},\eta)
be the cone of points
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying

\gamma_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{V}))+\gamma_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{\perp}(\mathbf{W}))+\sum_{i=o+1}^{n}\omega_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\sharp}\leq c_{0}\left[\gamma_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}))+\gamma_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(\mathbf{W}))+\eta\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}\right].

We will sometimes omit some of the subscripts when they are clear in the context.

The one-dimensional cone
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\mathcal{R}}(c_{0}):=\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{0},\mathcal{R},0}(c_{0},1,0,0)
is well known in high-dimensional statistics. In the analysis of RTRMD the three-dimensional cone
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}}
is useful. Next, we will need some additional notation when dealing with contamination in miss-specified models.

Definition 14
.

Let
\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
and non-negative numbers
(c_{0},\alpha,\mathsf{f},r)
. Define the quantities
R_{\mathcal{R},c_{0}}(\mathbf{V}|\mathbf{B}):=\Psi_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{V}))\mu(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\mathcal{R}}(2c_{0}))
and
R_{\mathcal{S},c_{0}}(\mathbf{W}|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}):=\Psi_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(\mathbf{W}))\mu(\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\mathcal{S}}(2c_{0}))
. Additionally, set

\displaystyle r_{\lambda,\chi,\alpha,c_{0}}(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}|\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma})

\displaystyle:=\{\lambda^{2}R_{\mathcal{R},c_{0}}^{2}(\mathbf{V}|\mathbf{B})+\chi^{2}R_{\mathcal{S},c_{0}}^{2}(\mathbf{W}|\boldsymbol{\Gamma})+\alpha^{2}\}^{1/2}.

(56)

Finally, set

\spadesuit_{2}(\mathsf{f},r):=(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathsf{d}_{1}^{2}}})\left(4\mathsf{f}+3r\right)^{2}

and

\clubsuit_{2}(\mathsf{f},r):=(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathsf{d}_{1}^{2}}})\left(16\mathsf{f}+12r\right).

Theorem 15

(
q=2
& matrix decomposition)
.

Grant Assumption
1
and model (
2
). Consider the solution
[\hat{\mathbf{B}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
of (
3
) with hyper-parameters

\lambda,\chi,\tau>0
and
\mathsf{a}\in(0,\infty]
. Let
\mathsf{c}_{*},\mathsf{f}_{*}\geq 0
be absolute constants and suppose:

(i)

(\mathfrak{X},\boldsymbol{\xi})
satisfies
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\mathsf{f}_{3},\mathsf{f}_{4})
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies
\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{3},\mathsf{d}_{4})
.

(iii)

\mathfrak{X}
satisfies
\IP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}\left(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{4}\right)
.

(iv)

The hyper-parameters
(\lambda,\chi,\tau)
satisfy
\tau\geq 4[(\sigma\mathsf{d}_{4})\vee(2\mathsf{f}_{4})]
,

\displaystyle\lambda\geq 4[(\sigma\mathsf{d}_{2})\vee(2\mathsf{f}_{2}+2\mathsf{c}_{*}\sigma\mathsf{b}_{2})]\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\chi\geq 4[(\sigma\mathsf{d}_{3})\vee(2\mathsf{f}_{3}+2\mathsf{f}_{*}+2\mathsf{c}_{*}\sigma\mathsf{b}_{3})].

(57)

(v)

\hat{\mathsf{f}}_{1}:=\mathsf{f}_{1}+\mathsf{b}_{1}(\mathsf{c}_{*}\sigma)+(\nicefrac{{2\mathsf{b}_{4}}}{{\tau}})(\mathsf{c}_{*}^{2}\sigma^{2})\leq\sigma\mathsf{d}_{1}/2
.

Let any
D\geq 0
and
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]
satisfying the constraints

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=D,

(58)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{a},

(59)

\displaystyle|\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}|

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{f}_{*}\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}),

(60)

\displaystyle r:=r_{\lambda,\chi,\tau\Omega,3}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}|\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma})

\displaystyle<\sigma\mathsf{d}_{1},

(61)

\displaystyle D^{2}+\spadesuit_{2}(\mathsf{f}_{1},r)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{c}_{*}^{2}\sigma^{2},

(62)

\displaystyle\left[(\nicefrac{{D^{2}}}{{\sigma\mathsf{d}_{1}}})\right]\bigvee\left[(\nicefrac{{2\sqrt{2}}}{{\mathsf{d}_{1}}})D+\clubsuit_{2}(\mathsf{f}_{1},r)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{c}_{*}\sigma.

(63)

Define the quantities

\hat{r}:=r_{\lambda,\chi,0,3}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}|\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
and

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{f}_{1}+\mathsf{b}_{1}\left((\nicefrac{{D^{2}}}{{\sigma\mathsf{d}_{1}}})\bigvee\left((\nicefrac{{2\sqrt{2}}}{{\mathsf{d}_{1}}})D+\clubsuit_{2}(\mathsf{f}_{1},r)\right)\right)+(2\mathsf{b}_{4}/\tau)\left(D^{2}+\spadesuit_{2}(\mathsf{f}_{1},r)\right).

(64)

Then, it holds that

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})\left(\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}})+\chi\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})\right)+\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\hat{\mathbf{B}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq D^{2}+\spadesuit_{2}(F,\hat{r}).

(65)

\displaystyle\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]\|_{\Pi}\leq(\nicefrac{{D^{2}}}{{\sigma\mathsf{d}_{1}}})\bigvee\left[(\nicefrac{{2\sqrt{2}}}{{\mathsf{d}_{1}}})D+\clubsuit_{2}(F,\hat{r})\right].

(66)

The conditions (i)-(iii) are the required design properties. Condition (iv) prescribes the “optimal” level of the hyper-parameters

(\lambda,\chi,\tau)
in terms of the design constants, the noise level and the low-spikeness constant
\mathsf{f}_{*}
. Notice that
(\lambda,\chi)
also depend on the constant
\mathsf{c}_{*}
— this constant is related with the constraints (
58
) and (
62
)-(
63
). As explained later, these constraints identify the effect of the corruption error
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
and miss-specification error on the choice of
(\lambda,\chi)
. The constraints (
59
)-(
60
) encode the low-spikeness assumption. Finally, condition (v) and constraint (
61
) encode the minimal sample size and maximum breakdown point. Notice that the corruption and miss-specification errors also impact condition (v) via the constant
\mathsf{c}_{*}
.

The proof of Theorem
15
will be done via intermediate lemmas, stated next. These are proven in the supplement. We start with the next lemma, a consequence of the first order condition of (
3
). In the following, we grant Assumption
1
and model (
2
) and set
\boldsymbol{\Delta}=\mathfrak{X}(\hat{\mathbf{B}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})-\boldsymbol{f}
.

Lemma 16
.

For all
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}
such that

\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathsf{a}
,

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{(n)}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}},\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)},\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\rangle

(67)

\displaystyle+\lambda\big(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\big)+\chi\big(\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\mathcal{S}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})\big)+\tau\big(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}\big).

(68)

Next, we upper (and lower) bound (
68
) using
\MP
(and
\ATP
). In case of additive matrix decomposition, we require an additional condition related to the spikeness assumption.

Lemma 17
.

Suppose conditions (i)-(ii) of Theorem
15
hold. For some
\mathsf{f}_{*}\geq 0
, let
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]
such that

\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathsf{a}
and

|\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}|\leq\mathsf{f}_{*}\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}).

Define the quantities

\displaystyle\blacktriangle

\displaystyle:=((\sigma\mathsf{d}_{2})\vee(2\mathsf{f}_{2}))\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}})+((\sigma\mathsf{d}_{3})\vee(2\mathsf{f}_{3}+2\mathsf{f}_{*}))\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})+((\sigma\mathsf{d}_{4})\vee(2\mathsf{f}_{4}))\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp},

(69)

\displaystyle\blacktriangledown

\displaystyle:=\lambda\big(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\big)+\chi\big(\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\mathcal{S}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})\big)+\tau\big(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}\big).

(70)

Then

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{(n)}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}^{2}+\left(\mathsf{d}_{1}\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}-(\nicefrac{{\blacktriangle}}{{\sigma}})\right)_{+}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}

(71)

\displaystyle+2\mathsf{f}_{1}\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}+\blacktriangle+2\blacktriangledown.

(72)

To illustrate, consider well-specified trace regression with label contamination — namely,
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}^{*}
,

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B}^{*})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}=\mathbf{0}
. In this case,
\chi=\mathsf{f}_{*}=0
,
\mathsf{a}=\infty
and it is sufficient that
\MP
and
\ATP
hold with
\mathsf{d}_{3}=\mathsf{f}_{3}=0
and
\mathcal{S}\equiv 0
. Using (
68
), a similar proof of (
72
) entails

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}+\left(\mathsf{f}_{2}+\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{c}_{2}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\right)\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}})+\left(\mathsf{f}_{3}+\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{c}_{3}}{\mathsf{c}_{1}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}

(73)

\displaystyle+\lambda\big(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\big)+\tau\big(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{\sharp}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{\sharp}\big).

(74)

In case
\mathsf{f}_{1}=0
, the above bound and decomposability of norms can be used to show that
[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}^{*}},\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*},\mathbf{0}}(c_{0},\gamma,0,\Omega)
for some
c_{0}>0
and
\gamma:=\lambda/\tau
— provided the penalization
(\lambda,\tau)
is large enough. This would be the approach using the dual-norm inequality. Instead, we resort to Theorem
6
to obtain
\MP
with
\mathsf{f}_{1}\neq 0
— enabling us to obtain the optimal rate in

\delta
.
\ATP
can be further used to lower bound (
74
) — as it implies restricted strong convexity over
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B}^{*},\mathbf{0}}(c_{0},\gamma,0,\Omega)
. Inequality (
72
) is a non trivial generalization of (
74
) to handle miss-specification and additive matrix decomposition.
19
19

19

More precisely, (
72
) gives a recursion in the variable
\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}
— instead of

\|\mathfrak{M}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\mathbf{B}}}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\|_{2}
. This technical point is needed in case of matrix decomposition, accounting for the “bias”

|\langle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\rangle\mathchoice{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-4.5mu}{{}\mkern-2.5mu}{}\rangle_{\Pi}|\leq\mathsf{f}_{*}\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}).

Remark 9

(Relevance of
\PP
and
\IP
)
.

One should not take for granted the fact that
\PP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{c}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{4})
and
\IP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}\left(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{4}\right)
over the triplet
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]
are
implicitly
invoked in Lemma
17
. Indeed, by Lemmas
9
and
11
, both properties entail

\ATP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{d}_{1},\mathsf{d}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{3},\mathsf{d}_{4})
. The optimal bound for
\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}
is obtained invoking
\ATP
with sharp constants (as stated in Proposition
2
). One could argue if a more “direct” approach could lead to the optimal rate, for instance, one without resorting to such technical definitions. It turns out that the mere use of dual-norm inequalities is suboptimal.

Before proceeding, we state the next lemma stating the useful bounds (
78
)-(
79
) for points in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
. For convenience, given
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\boldsymbol{u}]
, we define

\displaystyle\triangle_{\lambda,\chi,\tau}(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}|\mathbf{B},\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle:=(\nicefrac{{3\lambda}}{{2}})(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}})(\mathbf{V})-(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{2}})(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{B}}^{\perp})(\mathbf{V})

(75)

\displaystyle+(\nicefrac{{3\chi}}{{2}})(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})(\mathbf{W})-(\nicefrac{{\chi}}{{2}})(\mathcal{S}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{\perp})(\mathbf{W})

(76)

\displaystyle+(\nicefrac{{3\tau\Omega}}{{2}})\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}-(\nicefrac{{\tau}}{{2}})\sum_{i=o+1}^{n}\omega_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\sharp}.

(77)

Lemma 18
.

Define
\gamma_{\mathcal{R}}:=\lambda/\tau
and
\gamma_{\mathcal{S}}:=\chi/\tau
and let
c_{0},\eta>0
. Then, for any
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]
and
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(c_{0},\gamma_{\mathcal{R}},\gamma_{\mathcal{S}},\eta)
,

\displaystyle\triangle_{\lambda,\chi,\tau}(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}|\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma})

\displaystyle\leq(\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}})r_{\lambda,\chi,\tau\eta,c_{0}}(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}|\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\cdot\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi},

(78)

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{V})+\chi\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{W})+\tau\big\|\boldsymbol{u}\big\|_{\sharp}

\displaystyle\leq 2(c_{0}+1)\cdot r_{\lambda,\chi,\tau\eta,c_{0}}(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}|\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\cdot\|[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\|_{\Pi}.

(79)

The previous lemmas entail the next proposition.

Proposition 3
.

Suppose the conditions (i)-(ii) of Theorem
15
hold and, additionally,

(iii’)

\lambda\geq 4[(\sigma\mathsf{d}_{2})\vee(2\mathsf{f}_{2})],

\chi\geq 4[(\sigma\mathsf{d}_{3})\vee(2\mathsf{f}_{3}+2\mathsf{f}_{*})]

and

\tau\geq 4[(\sigma\mathsf{d}_{4})\vee(2\mathsf{f}_{4})].

(iv’)

2\mathsf{f}_{1}\leq\sigma\mathsf{d}_{1}
.

For any
D\geq 0
and
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]
satisfying the constraints
(
58
), (
59
), (
60
) and (
61
),

\displaystyle(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})(\lambda\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}})+\chi\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp})+\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{(n)}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq D^{2}+\spadesuit_{2}(\mathsf{f}_{1},r),

(80)

where
r:=r_{\lambda,\chi,\tau\Omega,3}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}|\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
.
Additionally,

\displaystyle\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\|_{\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq\left[(\nicefrac{{D^{2}}}{{\sigma\mathsf{d}_{1}}})\right]\bigvee\left[(\nicefrac{{2\sqrt{2}}}{{\mathsf{d}_{1}}})D+\clubsuit_{2}(\mathsf{f}_{1},r)\right].

(81)

Using Proposition
2
, we can show the bound in (
80
) is a near-optimal oracle inequality for the triplet

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]
. Still, it is suboptimal for

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
. Next, we show that the bounds for
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
, implied by Proposition
3
, are enough to obtain the near-optimal rate for

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
. First, we prove the following lemma — an easy consequence of the first-order condition for fixed

\boldsymbol{\theta}\equiv\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.

Lemma 19
.

For all
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}
such that
\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathsf{a}
,

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{(n)},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})\rangle

(82)

\displaystyle+\lambda(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}}))+\chi(\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\mathcal{S}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})).

(83)

In case of label contamination and/or additive matrix decomposition, a key difference with the standard “Lasso proof” is that
\MP_{\mathcal{R},0,0}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},0,0)
is not sufficient to upper bound (
83
). Indeed, the noise

\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)}
is shifted by
-\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
and the multiplier process depends on the decomposed error
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
. Suppose
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\mathsf{f}_{3},\mathsf{f}_{4})
and
\IP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{4})
hold. The next lemma states that, if the nuisance error
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
is “not too large” compared to the noise, then the “perturbed multiplier process”

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]\mapsto\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}},\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})\rangle

in (
83
) can be effectively upper bounded. For the lower bound, we assume

\ATP
and the low-spikeness condition hold.

Lemma 20
.

Suppose conditions (i)-(iii) of Theorem
15
hold. For some
\mathsf{f}_{*}\geq 0
, let
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}]
such that

\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathsf{a}
and

|\langle\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\rangle|\leq\mathsf{f}_{*}\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}).

Define the quantities

\displaystyle\hat{\blacktriangle}

\displaystyle:=\left((\sigma\mathsf{d}_{2})\vee\left(2\mathsf{f}_{2}+2\mathsf{b}_{2}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}\right)\right)\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}})+\left((\sigma\mathsf{d}_{3})\vee\left(2\mathsf{f}_{3}+2\mathsf{f}_{*}+2\mathsf{b}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}\right)\right)\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}),

(84)

\displaystyle\hat{\blacktriangledown}

\displaystyle:=\lambda\big(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})-\mathcal{R}(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\big)+\chi\big(\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\mathcal{S}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})\big).

(85)

Then

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}+\left(\mathsf{d}_{1}\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]\|_{\Pi}-(\nicefrac{{\hat{\blacktriangle}}}{{\sigma}})\right)_{+}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathfrak{X}^{(n)}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\boldsymbol{f}^{(n)}\|_{2}^{2}

(86)

\displaystyle+\left(2\mathsf{f}_{1}+2\mathsf{b}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}+2\mathsf{b}_{4}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}\right)\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]\|_{\Pi}

(87)

\displaystyle+\hat{\blacktriangle}+2\hat{\blacktriangledown}.

(88)

With no contamination (
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}=\mathbf{0}
,
\tau=0
), Lemmas
17
and
20
coincide. In that case, Proposition
3
implies the optimal bound for
[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
. Lemma
20
improves upon Lemma
17
in case of label contamination. Next, we discuss how this lemma is used in the proof of Theorem
15
. The complete proof is presented in the supplement.

Lemma
20
gives a recursion on the parameter error
[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
— instead of

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]
as in Lemma
17
. Instead of a parameter estimate,
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
is seen as a nuisance error perturbing the noise levels
(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\mathsf{f}_{3})
and
(\sigma\mathsf{d}_{1},\sigma\mathsf{d}_{2},\sigma\mathsf{d}_{3})
. As expected, this perturbation affects the tuning of the hyper-parameters

(\lambda,\chi)
and the corresponding rate for

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
. For (
88
) to be meaningful,

\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
must be small enough compared to the noise. Precisely, the auxiliary Proposition
3
implies that

\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}\leq\mathsf{c}_{*}\sigma
and

\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}\leq\mathsf{c}_{*}^{2}\sigma^{2}
, in case we assume (
62
)-(
63
). Using Proposition
2
, we can show that these conditions hold with high probability — including miss-specified models satisfying

(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\|\mathfrak{X}(\mathbf{B}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma})-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{2}
.

Remark 10

(The relevance of
\IP
)
.

\IP
over the triplet
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]
is a major tool in the proof of Lemma
20
. Examining this lemma, we see that the thresholds for
(\lambda,\chi)
are perturbed, respectively, by terms of order
\mathsf{b}_{2}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}
and

\mathsf{b}_{3}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}
and the coefficient of
\|[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]\|_{\Pi}
in inequality (
88
) is perturbed by a term of order
\mathsf{b}_{1}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{4}\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}
. The rate optimality of estimator (
3
) follows from these precise expressions and the bounds

\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{2}\leq\mathsf{c}_{*}\sigma
and

\tau\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\|_{\sharp}\leq\mathsf{c}_{*}^{2}\sigma^{2}
. Indeed, Proposition
2
reveals that the constants
(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{4})
are sharp — it can be shown that the mere use of dual-norm inequalities instead of
\IP
do not entail the optimal rate for
[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\mathbf{B}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}]
.

Remark 11

(Proofs in
[
27
]
)
.

[
27
]
studies well-specified robust sparse regression with Huber’s loss and Gaussian distributions. There are two “high level ideas” used in
[
27
]
which we borrow. Their first idea is to identify
\IP_{\|\cdot\|_{1},0,\|\cdot\|_{1}}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},0,\mathsf{b}_{4})
over the pair
[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
as the sufficient design property to handle label contamination with Huber’s loss — in case the parameter is known to be sparse and well-specified. To prove this property they use Chevet’s inequality for gaussian processes. Their second idea is to treat
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
as a nuisance parameter, using a “two-stage” proof. The first stage establishes the optimal bound for the pair

[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}]
. The second establishes the optimal bound for
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}}
, using the nuisance bound for
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
.

In this work, we study a broader model: miss-specified RTRMD. As such, the arguments in the proof of Theorem
15
have substantial changes, both on technical details and structural design properties. On a fundamental level, we give three contributions when compared to the analysis in
[
27
]
. The first is to identify the new design property
\PP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{c}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{2},\mathsf{c}_{3},\mathsf{c}_{4})
over the pair
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}
as the sufficient property to handle additive matrix decomposition in trace regression.

The second is to identify the more general version
\IP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{b}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{3},\mathsf{b}_{4})
over the triplet
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]\in(\mathds{R}^{p})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and its relation with
\PP
, as the sufficient design properties to handle, simultaneously, label contamination and additive matrix decomposition. For this, we fundamentally need to use Chevet’s inequality and the product process inequality of Theorem
7
. We are not aware of a similar application of product processes in high-dimensional estimation.

The proof in
[
27
]
handles the design-noise interaction in the simplest way: invoking
\MP_{\|\cdot\|_{1},0,\|\cdot\|_{1}}(0,\mathsf{f}_{2},0,\mathsf{f}_{4})
over the pair
[\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u}]
via the dual-norm inequality. Consequently, they do not attain the subgaussian rate. Our third contribution is to derive a multiplier process inequality (Theorem
6
) and obtain the general version
\MP_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S},\|\cdot\|_{\sharp}}(\mathsf{f}_{1},\mathsf{f}_{2},\mathsf{f}_{3},\mathsf{f}_{4})
over the triplet
[\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W},\boldsymbol{u}]
. It leads to sharp constants in terms of
\delta
when the noise is feature-dependent — recall the discussion in Section
9
and comparison with
[
6
]
. The
\MP
property with constant
\mathsf{f}_{1}\neq 0
is fundamental to achieve the optimal subgaussian rate in
\delta
with
\delta
-adaptive estimators.

11
Second general theorem

In what follows,
\mathcal{R}
is a decomposable norm in

\mathds{R}^{p}
. The main result of this section is Theorem
21
. To stated it, we fix the positive constants
\{\mathsf{a}_{i}\}
,
\{\mathsf{b}_{i}\}
,
\{\mathsf{d}_{i}\}
and
\{\mathsf{f}_{i}\}
in Definition
8
. Theorem
4
will follow from Theorem
21
and Proposition
2
. Before stating Theorem
21
, we simplify some of the notation in Definition
14
. Given
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{V}\in\mathds{R}^{p}
and
\alpha,c_{0}>0
, we define

r_{\lambda,\alpha,c_{0}}(\mathbf{V}|\mathbf{B}):=\{\lambda^{2}R_{\mathcal{R},c_{0}}^{2}(\mathbf{V}|\mathbf{B})+\alpha^{2}\}^{1/2}.

Given
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{c}>0
, we let

\spadesuit_{1}(\mathsf{f},\mathsf{c}):=(\nicefrac{{2}}{{\mathsf{d}_{1}^{2}}})\left(2\mathsf{f}+\mathsf{c}\right)^{2}

and

\clubsuit_{1}(\mathsf{f},\mathsf{c}):=(\nicefrac{{4}}{{\mathsf{d}_{1}}})\left(2\mathsf{f}+\mathsf{c}\right).

Theorem 21

(
q=1
& no matrix decomposition)
.

Grant Assumption

\displaystyle(1-4(\mathsf{c}_{n}+\mathsf{c}_{*}))\hat{\sigma}\lambda

\displaystyle\geq 4\left[\mathsf{f}_{2}+\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathsf{d}_{2}}{\mathsf{d}_{1}}+\left(\mathsf{b}_{2}+\frac{\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{d}_{2}}{\mathsf{d}_{1}}\right)\mathsf{c}_{*}\hat{\sigma}+2(\nicefrac{{\mathsf{b}_{4}}}{{\tau}})\frac{\mathsf{d}_{2}}{\mathsf{d}_{1}}\mathsf{c}_{*}^{2}\hat{\sigma}^{2}\right],

Theorem
21
has a similar format to Theorem
15
but with a few differences. The first obvious one is that constraints (
59
)-(
60
) are excluded. Indeed, Theorem

is not concerned with matrix decomposition (
\mathsf{a}=\infty

). The main difference is that

in condition (iv) of Theorem

substitutes

in condition (iv) of Theorem

. By Bernstein’s inequality,
\sigma/2\leq\hat{\sigma}\leq 3\sigma/2
with high probability — assuming
n\gtrsim\sigma^{2}(1+\log(1/\delta))
. This justifies why the tuning

(\lambda,\tau)
in Theorem

is adaptive to
\sigma
. Another difference is that the constraints (

) and (

)-(

) require the constants
(\mathsf{c}_{n},\mathsf{c}_{*})
to be

— recall (
25
). This justifies why the approximation error in Theorem
4
must be

\mathcal{O}(\sigma)r_{n,d_{\eff},\delta}(\rho,\mu_{*})
instead of
\mathcal{O}(\sigma)
as in Theorem
3
.

From the previous discussion, it is not surprising that the road map of the proofs of Theorem

and Theorem

are, respectively, an adaptation of the proofs of Theorem

and Theorem

in Section

Simulation results

We report simulation results in
R
with synthetic data demonstrating agreement between theory and practice. The code is in
https://github.com/philipthomp/Outlier-robust-regression
. We simulate with standard gaussian design entries and gaussian noise. In experiments j) and k) below,
\sigma=0.1
; in the others,we use

\sigma=1
. The purpose of our experiments is threefold. The first is to identify empirically the guarantees of Theorems
2
and
3
for the estimators (
3
) and (
3
) with
q=2
. Namely, the linear growth of the mean standard error
(
\sqrt{\texttt{MSE}}
) with respect to
\epsilon
and of the mean squared error (
MSE
) with respect to
(r,s)
. Secondly, we wish to verify empirically the superiority of “sorted” Huber regression to classical Huber regression. Thirdly, we compare robust estimators with non-robust regularized estimators. Our solvers use a batch version of an alternated proximal gradient method on the separable variables
[\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\boldsymbol{\theta}]
.
22
22

22

The proximal map of the Slope or
\ell_{1}
norms are computed with the function
sortedL1Prox()
of the
SLOPE
R package
[
11
]
. The
(
\ell_{\infty}
-constrained) proximal map of the nuclear norm is computed via (
\ell_{\infty}
-constrained) soft-thresholding of the singular value decomposition.
When using the Slope norm, we always set
A=\bar{A}=10
in the sequences
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
. “Huber” and “Sorted Huber” denote Huber regression and Sorted-Huber regression respectively. Due to lack of space, we leave to future work numerical results for the robust estimator (
3
) regarding adaptation to
\sigma
. We implement the following experiments:

a)

We conduct Sorted-Huber sparse linear regression with estimator (
3
) (
q=2
) with
p=100
,
n=1000
,
s=\{15,25,35\}
and 100 repetitions. We take
\mathcal{R}
to be the Slope norm in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Respectively,
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
are set with the first
s
and
o
entries equal to
10
and zero otherwise. See Figure
2
(a).

b)

Same set-up of a) but with
s=25
and parameter coordinates with modulus
50
. This time, we compare “Huber” and “Sorted Huber”. See Figure
1
. The first 25 entries estimated by “Huber” and “Sorted Huber” fluctuated around
40
and
